mr president, last saturday we lost the former prime minister of the state of israel, ariel sharon. i'd like to ask for a minute of silence for him. thank you, sir.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the netherlands we have a vibrating earth, in the north of the netherlands, in groningen. that's because of our big gas gains from the past and big gas production there. we were so excited, but now we're seeing the consequences. cracks in houses, massive damage and an earth that is shaking right now. we don't know how to stop this either. we've been looking at whether we can find a solution by maybe eventually making it a ccs project, or at least putting something back. but in the end it turns out that in the netherlands it is now so far: we don't want to do this. this has been clear before, for example in the bar. to put gas, co2, back into the soil, there's no public support for that. not under your schools, not under your own house, not even under your own village. so there is no public support. that's point one, and actually i think i need hardly mention anything else. but there are four other points. that is: who's in charge? yeah, well, that's pretty clear for now. but we have seen in recent years how fast europe is developing. so who's responsible for 50, 100, 250 years from now? thirdly, the investment in ccs is excessive. you're kind of creating a parallel universe of energy. if i may remind you for a moment: it took 150 years to get to this energy system that we have now, and it cost trillions of dollars. fourthly: every dollar we put into ccs is one too many. we could have put that into real renewable energy, the real future that we're all responsible for.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. whereas in 2009 there were 100 killings, that number tripled to 300 in 2010. and it became clear to me two years ago that it wasn't just about rhinos. it turned out to be about elephants, too. thirty to forty thousand elephants are now being butchered there each year. about ten percent of the total population. but not just these large animals, but tigers, armadillos, snakes, reptiles, birds, and even plants and trees. mr. president, one thing has become clear to me: our nature has been declared bird-free and is being completely plundered. the main cause is the increased prosperity in southeast asia. this has created a market of illegal wildlife products worth over 15 billion euros annually. this makes it the fourth largest illegal trade in the world. after drugs, counterfeit goods and human trafficking comes wildlife crime. to combat this, i drew up my own european action plan a year ago and submitted it to the european commission. i am glad that the european commission has taken elements of that over. among other things, that led to an agreement with china last year to work together to fight wildlife crime. but, mr president, that is far from enough. this is also reflected in the latest figures for 2013. unfortunately, wildlife crime has skyrocketed again last year. i was talking about 100 rhinos in 2009, 300 in 2010 in south africa. last year, there were a thousand. it's just kept growing, despite all the efforts of ngos, rangers on the ground, organizations like europol, interpol, unep, traffic and many, many others. so, mr president, the conclusion is clear. if we don't take radical action very soon to stop these illegal practices, we're going to end up with the most iconic species on earth in the wild. that is why i have taken the initiative of this oral question with resolution. the role of europe is also important. it's not just about africa and asia. i'll tell you why. first of all, we have a very strong moral obligation to combat this mass slaughter, and that is not unimportant. secondly - and this is a serious matter - our member states unfortunately also play a major role in this trade, both as destinations and as ports of transit. i'll give you some figures from 2012 in the netherlands. at one point, 600 kilos of armadillo were intercepted there, or 50 tusks in one shipment. there were 680 kilos of illegal coral in one package, and 2,500 dead seahorses. it also indicates that there are many different species involved. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. i would therefore like very much for the european commission to play a much greater role in this. thirdly, mr president, wildlife crime has a huge social impact. the disruptive effect is destabilizing entire regions. the easy money is attractive to many, as is the drug trade. it could make billions in development aid worthless. what's to be done? first of all, i think it is important that the european commission, together with the member states, should come up with its own action plan, which should be concrete and ambitious. as i said, expertise and capacity must be developed in the member states, both for investigation and for the judiciary. and with much higher penalties. criminals are getting away with it too easily. we must treat this crime as organized crime and deal with it in the same way as we deal with drug trafficking and many other organized crimes. much higher priority. we must set up a special wildlife crime unit within europol. that's how serious it is. it's absolutely necessary. we must use european trade and development policy to combat wildlife crime. condition aid through development instruments on contributions to combating wildlife crime. the resolution also calls on the commission and the member states to set up a trust fund to protect protected areas. president - and i shall end with this - what are we worth as politicians if we watch the plundering of our nature, of our most imaginative plant and animal species, but also the many less imaginative species that are disappearing because criminals are making billions from it. nothing, mr president, and let 2014 be the year that not only talks about wildlife crime, but also takes concrete action. i assume that the european commission and the member states will agree with this parliament and will come up with a concrete and ambitious action plan.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his answers to our questions. on the one hand, i am pleased with what the commissioner is saying. there will be a communication, communication with other commissioners, there will be a public consultation. it's all very beautiful, but i wonder if it's not too little and too slow. the 10th of april, a public consultation. between now and april 10th, that's over three months, that's 10,000 elephants. that is what we are talking about, that urgency, that is what we are talking about. how long can we afford to be so slow and unsteady in dealing with this? i understand the good intentions of commissioner potočnik. but i have the impression that we really need to take this to a higher plane. you yourself mentioned the united states. they're an example of that. it was hillary clinton as secretary of state who put this on the national agenda. it was even president obama who said this during his visit to africa. in the united states, i have the impression that it is given a much greater urgency. not only because they are so concerned about wildlife in the world, but also because they realize that this has a huge social impact in africa, in asia and in many other parts of the world, and also because this is becoming a giant criminal organization that is out of control. so, mr president, i would ask the commissioner to make it even more urgent to ensure that not only does he sign a memorandum of understanding with his chinese counterpart, but that the president of the european commission, mr barroso, discusses this with the president of china, with the presidents and prime ministers in africa and in other countries where this is on the agenda. i also feel that we should be much more inclined towards the european consumer. why not follow the example of other countries and call on the member states to publicly burn all the illegal ivory we have collected? that is such an important signal to consumers and they will understand that it is indeed not possible to import ivory from countries. mr president, yesterday i read that the lion in west africa is extinct, that the last elephants are roaming in the same part of africa. we really have very, very little time left. i would therefore ask the commission to give it much more urgency. in 2009, you yourself made recommendations to the member states. i have the impression - and this was also shown by the answers to my written questions - that the member states are doing far too little, far too little capacity, far too little national priority, both in the field of detection and in the field of justice, to ensure that europe is no longer the transit country and destination for a great many of these illegal wildlife products.
- i'm the reporter. - mr president, while most of my colleagues are obviously at dinner, i would like to draw your attention very briefly to the important issue of food fraud, which we are voting on tomorrow. of course, i would first of all like to thank the other groups, the shadow rapporteurs in particular, for the good cooperation they have shown so far. that led to support for the drafting of this report and also to the unanimous adoption of this report in the committee on the environment and public health. this is the first time that the european parliament has addressed the phenomenon of food fraud in this report, following a number of scandals, including the horse meat scandal last year. the first problem you run into is the lack of comparable data to make a good estimate of the exact scale of the problem. we know from academic studies - the few available - from europol interventions and from discussions with the sectors concerned which products are concerned, such as meat, olive oil, wine, organic products, but even coffee and tea are mentioned. we also know that billions of people in the european union are involved in the phenomenon of food fraud, and we are getting signs that organized crime is also beginning to take an interest in it. but there is a great need to get a better picture of the problem, and that is our first demand. for example, unlike the united states, commissioner, the european union does not yet have a common definition of food fraud. and it needs to be there soon to build on that. the report provides a first stimulus to this end. in short, the fight against food fraud in the european union is still in its infancy and has long been a blind spot for national and european authorities. especially those cases of fraud which did not have a direct impact on public health. of course, the protection of the health of europeans is and remains the highest priority. but other cases of food fraud also call for attention, because these rotten apples are ruining the lives of the vast majority of farmers, of businesses, of intermediaries who do follow the rules. these rotten apples are also destroying consumer confidence in food and nutrition information. in my own member state, the netherlands, for example, one third of consumers say they no longer trust food labels. i think that's a serious matter for the whole sector. the risk of food fraud is partly determined by the chance of getting caught and the potential profit that can be made from fraud. so the odds should be higher and the punishment should be more deterrent. i will give you the example of the horse meat scandal in ireland. four companies were involved. three companies committed the fraud just outside the irish border. that leaves one company the irish could potentially sue. the maximum fine, if they can prosecute that company, is then 30 000 euros, whereas with one container of horse meat relabelled as beef the profit is 65 000 euros. i think that the irish example shows very well what needs to change in the european union. first, tougher penalties. as far as we're concerned, at least twice the potential profit you can make from fraud. and people who make mistakes over and over again, we're gonna lose their license. second, we need to make controls smarter. and this is not just a question of more money, although we are very concerned about the cuts that are being made in the control services in the member states from time to time. but it also requires a different approach, less predictable, a more police-like approach than an official approach, as is already the case in denmark and italy, for example. improved crossborder cooperation also through europol, through a rapid alert system of the european commission. the 28 pieces of the national control puzzle need to fit together much better to give a single picture across europe. improved information flows through the production chain, especially among traders and intermediaries. but also, of course, the responsible business of supermarkets. telling people to do it cheaper doesn't always translate into quality. i would like to conclude by saying something about the origin labelling of meat, because that is going to be a big part of the debate. and the report rightly says that origin labelling is not in itself a means of combating fraud. in fact, you might be able to cheat on that origin labeling. but on the other hand, it might make people think more about where their ingredients come from, especially a fragile product like meat. so the report says: the commission, the european parliament and the council have all agreed that the commission should also make proposals on meat as an ingredient, but that this should be done in the light of an impact assessment and without any administrative burden. i think that the report is balanced overall and i look forward to the reactions of my colleagues and the vote tomorrow.
in writing. the pvv believes that consumer information and education are not a task for the european union. national consumer organisations are well placed to provide this if necessary. in addition, they can cooperate bilaterally on cross-border issues. this regulation provides subsidies for consumer information (such as comparative price or product information), which can be well organised by the market itself. enforcement and supervision are also eligible. however, as far as the pvv is concerned, these must be regarded as a regular task of supervisors and therefore a subsidy for this is absurd. the pvv is opposed to the proposal for these reasons.
in writing. − this report deals with the important issue of labour inspections, which are essential to the enforcement of rules on working conditions. it is important that member states cooperate, for example to combat social dumping, but it should be noted that labour inspection is not an eu competence. it is the responsibility of the member states to enforce proper working conditions. i vote in favour of the report on the assumption that it will not lead to more eu competences in this field.
president. - the commission's package of measures on public procurement aims to achieve a number of objectives, including efficiency in the spending of public funds. the pvv has a useful suggestion for achieving this efficiency: abolish concession and procurement policies and regulations. let the member states decide how and by whom they want their contracts, public works and utilities to be carried out. the rules only lead to more bureaucracy and more costs for the taxpayer. in addition, this proposal is crafty in imposing a political agenda of climate hysteria, diversity policies and other socialist nonsense on business owners. governments may take these aspects into account in their procurement procedures. the commission, the citizens want to pay for their tax money and certainly not to participate in more nonsense which is only useful to officials.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. - thank you, mr president, and thank you, commissioner de gucht. my suggestion to the committee on development and cooperation was to send this proposal back to the commission in its entirety. my proposal did not pass, but i will argue here why i think it should be returned. you could say that public procurement is part of free trade. i would argue that public procurement is also a way for a country to stimulate its own activity. and if i look at the agreements that countries around the world have concluded on the effectiveness of development cooperation (paris, accra, busan), public procurement is also used as a tool to help the home market. i understand that you want to open up the markets in china, brazil and india to european companies, and i have no problem with that either. but you could argue the other way around. it is the european union which has singlehandedly opened its own public procurement market to companies from outside the union. and it now seems that a coalition is being formed here in this parliament between those who want to bid on the chinese market - which i have no problem with - and those who think: this is a great way to close the european market again. but those who are caught between the rock and the hard place are the developing countries. the definition used in this proposal is that of developing countries. china and brazil and india are still, by the oecd definition, developing countries. but so are cameroon, fiji, ivory coast, guatemala and the gaza strip. they fall under the same definition. so in future they will not be able to say 'no thank you, i will help my own companies first', when it is only theoretically possible for them to bid on our market. that has to be resolved before we have a proposal that can be used.
madam president, although we are not obliged to do so through the wto gpa agreements, the eu chooses openness to our markets, including public procurement, and that makes sense. other countries are, sadly, not always acting in the spirit of reciprocity towards us, and this impacts on our businesses, whether large or small. it may be tempting to some to answer protectionist measures such as ‘buy american’ with ‘buy french’ or ‘buy european’, but we must resist such a race to the bottom, where one protectionist measure leads to another. instead of being reactive, together we must be proactive. this means we have to act strongly together and leverage the european single market while seeking free trade and access, also for our businesses, to public procurement globally. the amendments before us are the start of a process in which we must think broadly about the appropriate trade defence instruments while striving for global growth and development and trade. we are, of course, particularly aware of the importance of public procurement and reciprocity in our dealings and negotiations with the united states over the transatlantic trade and investment partnership.
mr de gucht, mr caspary and all the other members, we have a real problem. we want the public procurement market to be open. in europe it is also open, but there is no reciprocity everywhere. in some countries, it's just sitting there. so that means that where we are denied access, we have to look for solutions. i think that the commission has done a very creative thing. but i have my doubts. - why? - why? if the commission is going to decide to exclude a supplier on the basis of the origin of goods, i wonder whether we might not also be putting our own companies at a disadvantage, because our own companies are connected to companies elsewhere by the global value change. and i have a feeling that what we have agreed now, whatever is in the amendments, may not work. i also have some questions about the administrative burden that might result. because if you have to indicate the obligation of this added value, then you could also say that it could lead to protectionism. i have a further question on article 6. if you allow the decentralized authorities to say to the commission whether or not someone should be excluded, you are in effect decentralizing a piece of your foreign policy. i give everything the benefit of the doubt, but i do have a lot of questions.

mr president, the tachograph was once used as an aid to enforcement of the driving and resting time laws. the law aims to improve road safety and to prevent exploitation. there are various road offences. the new tachograph, however, will not solve these problems. and what's perhaps even worse is that this device is a gross violation of privacy. the device almost permanently registers the driver's location and, in the event of an emergency, can be remotely monitored and read. 'big brother eu is watching you!' this proposal is a very visible interference with the personal life of professional drivers. this is the conclusion of the european data protection supervisor. road safety is of the utmost importance, but the digital tachograph is not the answer.
