vice-president of the commission. - madam president, this evening's debate to approve the document under consideration, together with the text that will be approved, shows how much europe has changed compared to five years ago. this legislature with the present commission has certainly reversed a trend and given europe a new industrial policy strategy. this is not nostalgia for industrial policy in the 1900s, let alone the 1800s: it is a new way of focusing on growth with a modern, competitive, clean, high-quality industrial network, focusing on key sectors such as biotechnology, nanotechnology. a comprehensive industrial policy, thanks also to the support we have had - and i thank mr bütikofer for having emphasized this in his text - from the competitiveness council, which has become a sort of real industry council. we have adopted a number of initiatives, legislative and non-legislative, which have established a general framework for european industrial policy, the objectives we set ourselves, the overall strategy but also the actions to be taken sector by sector. i am thinking of cars 2020, i am thinking of the steel action plan, i am thinking of the compatible construction action plan, i am thinking of the shipyard action plan, leadership 2020, i am thinking of the action plan for an important industrial sector such as tourism, which is not talked about enough, i am thinking of the innovation partnership, for innovation, recycling and substitution of raw materials, with a strong european raw materials policy. mr bütikofer knows how much effort has gone into providing europe with such a strategy, in perfect symbiosis between the commission and the european parliament. but industrial policy means defending industry as a whole, and when i say industry i also mean the workers employed in it, because for the first time, also when the steel plan was presented, the commission told mr mittal to wait for europe's political choices before deciding on closures, before sending workers home, and thanks to the action of the european union, we have managed to prevent degeneration and thus to make industry wait for the political choices of the european union. we are also working to defend tenneco's asturian workers. because i'm totally against what tenneco is doing, because we can't close offices when they're making a lot of money ― and tenneco is making a lot of money―; we cannot look to invest in russia. i am against it, and i say it here in front of the european parliament: it is also industrial policy to defend workers. when you win, you don't close the shop. ...and we will do so for all industrial sectors where there will be an attempt to make money without looking at social policy, because the pole star that drives this european industrial policy is called the social market economy: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that, ladies and gentlemen, is what i believe we have changed in the last five years. of course, there's a lot to do. we must work to implement all the action plans, to give new rules, to ensure that there is an industrial policy that will achieve in the next few years the 20% target for which we are fighting for of gross domestic product from manufacturing. there will be much to do, much to commit to. but i believe that this is the best recipe for creating jobs, for getting out of the crisis once and for all and for creating, in the future, an economic fabric which will be able to withstand any future crises. because the european regions with a strong industrial and entrepreneurial fabric - and when i speak of industrial policy i always mean small and mediumsized enterprises too - are those which have withstood the shock of the crisis better. that is why i am firmly convinced that europe needs this new industrial revolution, which focuses on energy saving, which also focuses on new renewable energies, which focuses on a more modern energy policy, an energy policy that is more european. of course, we cannot expect all of europe to be industrialized in the same way, but we must certainly ensure that there is an entrepreneurial fabric, including internationalisation, to prevent relocation. that is why i firmly believe that this evening's debate and the document you are about to adopt - i believe this is the third debate on industrial policy during this parliamentary term, plus the many others we have had on the various sectors - represent an extraordinary political success because finally, after years and years, europe is once again believing in manufacturing, europe is once again believing in the real economy with its policy. that is why i think the message that is being sent out today by the european parliament and tomorrow by the vote on this document is important. but the decisions of the european commission next week will also send out further messages to the heads of state and government to ensure that they are strongly implemented and are increasingly convinced of the need to continue to implement this industrial policy, which is essential to create jobs and give hope to our young people.
he is seeking the presidency of the council. - the ladies and gentlemen of the european parliament, as a former colleague, i am honoured and excited to be here before you today, on the occasion of the fifth greek presidency. i am speaking today to you, who embody the common conscience of european citizens and the common will of our peoples to move towards european integration, to make europe a reality, to make europe a reality. i am speaking as president of the european council, the institution which expresses the sovereign will of the member states to move forward with the unification of europe. i would also like to pay tribute to the european commission, the institution which has implemented and monitors european integration in all its aspects and in all its details on a daily basis. we have the conscience of the european union, which is you. we have the sovereign will and the basic design of european integration which is the council. and we have the implementing mechanism, which is the commission. the soul, the mind and the muscle of the european idea. there may have been times when these three pillars were not in perfect harmony with each other. i did. but we wouldn't have gotten where we are if they hadn't found a way to work together and balance each other. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that is what we will work for and that is what we will strengthen. and that's my first commitment to you. as someone who was just a few years ago one of you, i know your agonies. i've had my own anxieties. i know your ambitions. i had my own ambitions. the european parliament must be strengthened. to be able to control decisions and to put the needs and interests of the european citizen at the heart of decisions. we all want a strong and responsible european parliament. we're proud that his influence has grown. greece as a country has supported this for years. and now we are confident that he will use his increased powers in the best way to bring the ideals of a united europe closer to the european citizens. i'm still talking to you at a very critical moment. the united europe has been tested in recent years. its cohesion has been tested, its strength has been tested, even the entire structure of the currency's public has been tested. and there were significant "construction shortfalls". but now that crisis is beginning to be overcome. not by herself. not automatically the crisis is overcome because europe has begun to overcome its weaknesses, has seen its mistakes, and has begun to correct them. we have thus proved, during and because of the crisis, that a united europe works. the changes we have initiated in the last two years must be completed. and we can go even further. the crisis has shown us that we need 'more europe and better europe'! that's what we do. and this is my next commitment to you: in the next period, we must go even further, even more decisively, so that in the european elections next may, which coincide with the end of the greek presidency, european citizens do not vote with the bitter taste of the crisis. but with the feeling, with the hope, that the crisis is over and that europe is coming out stronger and better. ladies and gentlemen, dear friends, i speak to you finally, and as prime minister of greece, the country that has been tested the most by this crisis. my country has been tested in recent years as perhaps no other member state of the union has ever been tested. she was tested by her own mistakes that had been going on for decades. it has even been tested because of the manufacturing weaknesses of the single currency itself. finally, it was tested by errors made in the design of the first programme, such as the 'multiplier' error which underestimated the impact of the first programme on the subsequent recession. and it turned out to be much bigger. greece was tested, but it held out! and now she's changing radically, overcoming her problems and entering a path of healing and recovery. i know that a lot has been said in this chamber, both positive and negative, about this period. let's just keep one thing in mind here: greece has kept its commitments and honoured its signature. in other words: we delivered! and i want to give you my personal experience: failure, my friends, is not about falling down. failure is not getting back up! and if when you fall, you get back up with tenacity, with faith and with your head held high, that's a special achievement. and i am proud that my country, through the unimaginably painful sacrifices of our people, is today back on its feet. so i'll say two words to you right now: in the last few months, greece has achieved the biggest fiscal correction ever in the shortest time: it has managed, in four years, to reduce its deficit by 13% of its current gdp! but to do this, it suffered a 25% decline in its gdp, the largest gdp decline ever seen internationally since the great depression of the 1930s! in six consecutive years of recession, the average greek has lost about 38% of his living standard compared to 2007. unemployment has risen from 7% to over 27%. and among young people under 25, it reached 60%. i really hope that this drama will never be experienced by another country. but more importantly, in the midst of such conditions of prolonged recession, greece embarked on one of the most ambitious, one of the most sweeping reform and structural change programs ever. in the oecd international ranking, greece is already ranked first internationally in the implementation of structural changes. and the results were: according to all surveys, greece has already regained all the competitiveness it lost since 2002, and has also brought its trade balance deficit to zero. in 2013, for the first time, greece posted a primary budget surplus. it has turned a surplus on its current account for the first time in many years. and 2014 is forecast to see the first recovery after six years of recession. we are under no illusion. we've come a long way, but we still have a long way to go. we have a lot to do. but we have succeeded in stabilizing the country, its economy and its society. and to give you an idea of the size and importance of what we have achieved, it is enough to say that today greece has the largest structural primary surplus in europe and in the eurozone. what is meant by 'structural primary surplus'? it means that if greece were to seize its economic potential, it could not only cover its current needs - which it already does - but also cover its borrowing interest and have a surplus every year to reduce its debt. and that's what we're trying to do now: accelerate sustainable growth from now on, so that we get rid of all need for support. and so that we can successfully complete the structural changes. because reforms are much easier in conditions of recovery and growth than in conditions of prolonged recession. which we're fortunately overcoming now. all this, of course, was achieved thanks to the unprecedented sacrifices and maturity of the greek people. but we also succeeded, thanks to the support of our partners. and from this position, i want to thank the people of europe for the solidarity they have shown to greece. i would like to thank the european parliament in particular. i would also like to thank president barroso, with whom we have been in constant contact during the most difficult hours of this project. and so greece avoided the disorderly default and achieved the largest debt reduction ever without default. this is a european achievement, and no one should underestimate it. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. but i have been working to correct those mistakes ever since. and then, as a government, we fixed it to a great extent. and so, while the programme seemed to be failing in the early years, now its key objectives are not only being met, but sometimes even exceeded. as was the case with the primary surplus planned for 2014 and already achieved since 2013. ladies and gentlemen, dear friends, a year and a half ago, my country was on the brink of disaster, and many people were speculating - and some were taking it for granted - that it would leave the euro. some even predicted that this would lead to the break-up of the eurozone. some went so far as to declare that the european union would also be dissolved! we've disproved all the cassandras! today the picture is completely different. greece has shown that europe works. that europe can. that our union is strong. and we prove it in the most compelling way: by overcoming our crises and coming out stronger from them. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. today greece is taking over the presidency of the union and i believe that in this way greece is becoming the symbol that europe is resilient, that europe is moving forward, that europe is working, that europe can. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. we shall proceed first of all with everything that has already been started. and we will put new issues on the european agenda. all of this can be summed up in four major priorities: first, to stimulate growth and employment. because prolonged unemployment undermines the cohesion of the societies most affected. but it also threatens the cohesion of the whole of europe! because when countries are at a standstill, they have the illusion that everyone can save themselves. that is precisely what we must fight. together we can achieve much more and much faster. and this 'all together' requires that we get out of the logic of prolonged stagnation. because the recession is tearing apart, not uniting. we must also strike a balance between stabilisation and sustainable growth. i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals, but i will say that the commission has already made a number of proposals. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information it receives. - long-term development is not the opposite of fiscal discipline. one is a prerequisite for the other. - in the long run, competitiveness is not the enemy of social justice. one cannot exist without the other. the precise framework for promoting growth, employment and cohesion is as follows: - the mid-term review of the europe 2020 strategy, which will start in the first quarter of 2014; - the pact for growth and jobs adopted by the european council in june 2012; - the deepening of the single market; - the new multiannual financial framework; and - the coordination of economic policies in the context of the european semester, in which the role of the european parliament and national parliaments is being significantly expanded. the greek presidency will give priority to promoting financing for the real economy, with a particular focus on smes. the commission's joint initiative with the european investment bank, with specific alternatives for financing european smes, has the full support of the greek presidency. we will do everything we can to help this initiative succeed, with the aim of improving growth rates and job-intensive projects in sectors such as transport, energy, shipping and tourism. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a very good one. financial initiatives to combat unemployment, such as the youth guarantee, are an important but not sufficient means of preventing this risk. at the same time, the debate on the long-term financing of the european economy must continue. a dialogue that started in march 2013 with the european commission's 'green paper'. the greek presidency will also promote the objectives of the 'growth and employment pact', including the completion of the single market, the fight against tax evasion and tax fraud, the advancement of innovation, the improvement of the regulatory framework in the union and the strengthening of external trade. secondly, to support our common currency and to increase confidence in the european economy. this means deepening emu, with the main immediate step being the completion of the banking union. we will work intensively with you in the trilogue to achieve the adoption of the single resolution mechanism (srm), which will break the vicious circle between banking and public debt, without compromising the single and european character of the mechanism. the european union has a long history of being a major player in the world economy. already yesterday, i have the pleasure of telling you that we had a first success: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we must not forget that a sound banking system is not just about 'bankers', it's about depositors, it's about investors, it's about providing the liquidity that every economy needs. but also the trust that's necessary for liquidity. finally, it is directly related to both competitiveness and employment. these are not all different, nor are they all competing with each other. they are different aspects of what we call 'more and better europe'. the european union is a europe of stability, confidence and growth. thirdly, the response we must give to the problem of migration, the tragic dimension of which is revealed almost daily at europe's sea and land borders. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. it may concern some of us more: the countries on the geographical borders of the union. but it's about all of us. the principles which should govern our approach are solidarity between partners, shared responsibility for the protection of the common european borders, proportionality in burden-sharing and, of course, the involvement of third countries, from which migration flows originate or through which they pass to reach us. because the phenomenon must be effectively tackled at its source. and that includes measures to repatriate illegal immigrants, those who are not from war zones and are not entitled to asylum. but here we have an additional humanitarian task. we must strike at the criminal networks which are smuggling, and this is the most tragic aspect of this case, people from different parts of the world to europe as if they were objects. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. member states and institutions are already working to strengthen and implement them. but they need to be redefined, they need to be reviewed, so that we can respond in the best possible way to the needs of citizens and to the challenges of the future. the two axes on which we are going to work are development, including the energy sector, and security, including the definition and management of european maritime zones. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. and i remind you of what has been heard in our chambers since 2004, when i was a member of the european parliament: europe must finally respond to the problem of energy insecurity. i would also like to mention something which has always been a central theme of european policy: european enlargement! the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the new regulation to include the areas of the environment, the environment and the environment policy. and here we are interested in being practical and efficient. the accession process of the countries of this region has gained a clear momentum over the last two years, thanks to the accession of croatia, the opening of accession negotiations with montenegro and the granting of candidate country status to serbia. these developments show that enlargement is proceeding, and that it is taking place in a period of transition for the union. the greek presidency will therefore approach enlargement dynamically and on a case-by-case basis, with the aim of promoting the accession process of each of the countries concerned, according to their performance and specificities. i would remind you that the basic principles and mechanisms of the 'thessaloniki agenda' continue to guide the region's path towards european integration. the european agenda for the coming months will include strengthening our relations with europe's major strategic partners, both in the west, across the atlantic, and in the east and the far east. here, our economic relations with them must be given a new impetus. the fight against recession is not the only reason for this, but it is also the reason why europe must play a leading role in international developments by helping to shape global trends. still, our influence in our circle. we need a region of stability, peace and development. where we will radiate our values and principles. where we can offer our help to those who need it. but we will also enjoy, together with our neighbours, the benefits of stability and peace. so above all we need two things: the european union is a great example of the european union's ability to act. and to become the beacon of stability and growth, of competitiveness and democracy, of civilization and prosperity. after all, that's what the rest of the world wants from us. the greek presidency will certainly not do these two things in the next six months. we have to do it together in the next few years. to stimulate europeans' pride in their union and to enhance the international profile of the european example. dear friends, allow me to conclude in english. as a political union, a single market and even an aid donor, europe is a superpower with a global vision. of course, sometimes we are all consumed in our everyday problems or disputes, thus losing both the big picture and our optimism. let us distinguish the essential from the trivial. let us look back at our past and realize how far we have come. deep in their hearts, the europeans always wanted to come together, one way or another, but they often ended up in bloody wars among themselves. in recent decades we have undertaken the most ambitious endeavour in human history: to have so many and such different peoples converge, of their own free will, in freedom and democracy. two generations ago, the dream of a united europe seemed unthinkable. one generation ago, this dream still seemed difficult and distant. yet it has now been realised to a greater extent than anyone ever expected. next, let us look inside ourselves: small states and big states alike, we all know that in the contemporary world we can achieve much more, and achieve it much faster, if we are united. all of us together can unlock our economic depth and our geopolitical weight and can play the leading worldwide role europe deserves. all of us together can better safeguard our security, protect our democracy, advance the prosperity of our peoples and project our cultural values to the rest of the world. our union is not a luxury. it is a vital necessity for all of us. lastly, let us look around us. we see turmoil emerging and major challenges appearing, almost everywhere. we can better respond to such challenges if we are united. we can better stand in the face of world turmoil if we grow even more united. to put it bluntly: united, we can export stability and security from europe to the rest of the world. separated, we will only import insecurity and destabilization from the rest of the world into our own house. no matter which way we look at it, no matter where we look for guidance or for inspiration - within ourselves or around us, back in our history or ahead of us in the future - what we need is more europe and a better europe. the best response to the current crisis is again: more europe and better europe. and the rest of the world also expects from us more europe and better europe. a united europe is not just some administrative bodies, some institutions or the bureaucratic procedures and technically complicated regulations. primarily, 'europe' is us 'all of us': everybody in this chamber, all european citizens who voted for us and sent us here. all of us, debating and sometimes disagreeing; all of us working hard for europe and enjoying the fruits of the union. europe is the heavy historical heritage which we all, collectively, carry on our shoulders. europe is the common cultural root and expression of our societies, the values of freedom and human dignity, social justice and compassion for all, progress and democracy across borders. europe is the future of the generations to come, of our children and those who will come after them. it is precisely this common future that we are shaping today. and it is this future that unites us, perhaps more than anything else. we are moving ahead step by step. sometimes we make mistakes, pause, correct them and take it from there. but we have gone far enough ? we have progressed more than any of the previous generations could imagine. by overcoming our recent crisis, we are moving even more decisively. remember the example of my country. greece suffered, and is still suffering? but we are now standing on our feet we are proud, we are holding our ground and are returning to normality, and by doing so we are proving to everyone that europe works and that europe can make it. by defending our destiny as a nation, we have also defended the future of europe, every step of the way. dear friends, europe started as a legend about a beautiful princess, seduced and kidnapped by an ancient greek god, who fell in love with her and transformed himself into a white bull to approach her. then this legend evolved into an epic story, full of glory as well as drama. and then, europe evolved as the cradle of human history, the source of culture and civilization across the continents. now it is transforming itself yet again: through inspiration and innovation, trial and error, successes and failures as is always the case in history. now europe is being transformed, not just into a common institutional framework for all its countries, but into a common identity and a common conscience for all its peoples. so does europe move ahead. not without problems, not without obstacles to jump over. but make no mistake: our united europe moves forward, to its destiny. and you, its elected representatives, are europe's heart and europe's soul. (applause from the left)
president of the commission. − mr president, prime minister, honourable members, first of all let me very sincerely congratulate you, prime minister, for your very inspiring words. listening to you i thought that europe not only owes much to ancient and classical greece – without greece, european civilisation would simply not exist as such – but that we are also going to owe a lot to modern greece for the great example of courage, determination and commitment to our europe that you personally, and the people of greece, are setting these days. it is, i believe, a fitting coincidence that we end this parliamentary term under the presidency of greece – a country that has been the focus of so much political energy and public attention during this mandate and that, in the end, has emboldened us to do all we can to fight both the root causes and the social consequences of the crisis. let us not forget that some people, not so long ago, were predicting that greece would exit the euro and that greece could even leave the european union. in the more apocalyptic scenarios, some people were even predicting the implosion of the euro and, eventually, the disintegration of the european union. the fact that today the prime minister of greece, with his strong european commitment and pride in the commitment of his country to europe, is here standing among us, launching this new presidency, proves that the prophets of doom were wrong. the resilience and determination of the greek people are far, far greater than the scaremongers were ready to foresee. greece’s own experiences since the beginning of the crisis make it even more determined to make a success of this presidency. the commission and the greek presidency are united in not being distracted from the real agenda we have in front of us. we must stay focused on delivering concrete results for the growth and jobs which europe’s citizens need. greece has embarked on a very painful, but necessary, adjustment process, and some impressive results are already appearing: a government deficit that has been cut from nearly 15 % in 2009 to an expected primary surplus in 2013; recovering competitiveness, as illustrated by the steadily-falling current account deficit; and a vast programme of structural and public sector reforms. we expect positive growth throughout this year in greece. the courage of the greek people in the face of great hardship is bearing fruit and, as you well know, prime minister, the european commission has always stood by greece and the greek people. i thank you for your kind words recognising that. as you also know, when many others were expressing doubts and less commitment to greece, this parliament also always stood by greece and supported greece. honourable members, as i already told prime minister samaras in athens, the commission fully shares greece’s priorities: supporting growth and jobs through the deepening of the single market and boosting external trade; deepening the economic and monetary union and reinforcing the social dimension; improving the framework for human mobility and addressing the real problem of illegal migration; and promoting maritime issues. the list is long and the ambition is great, but based on the extensive experience of greece – this is the fifth presidency of the council by greece, and i at least have worked with three presidencies – i believe that, with the strong determination of the prime minister and the government, it will be a success. the focus of the presidency, strongly shared by the commission and the greek government, for the time left before the end of this legislative term is clear: we need to finish what we started and to deliver as much of our growth and jobs agenda as we possibly can. consistent and continued structural reform is the way to do this. whereas only a year ago commentators – and not only commentators but some politicians too – were predicting that countries would exit the euro area, around the turn of the year we started to see programme countries exiting their respective support schemes. ireland is now able to issue long-term debt at rates of only about 3 %, which is less than some other countries that did not need to ask for adjustment programmes and assistance. latvia, which – together with greece – has implemented one of the toughest adjustment programmes, has not only joined the euro area but currently has the highest growth rate in the european union. spain is exiting its specific programme for banks and is once again showing its remarkable dynamism and attracting new investors. portugal has seen positive growth since the second quarter of last year, and its unemployment rate and interest spreads are consistently going down. so do not tell me that structural reform is unnecessary or unproductive: the facts show otherwise. we need structural reforms so that we can address the real problems of competitiveness in many of our countries if we do not want to be losers of globalisation. yet we never said that this was only about fiscal consolidation or structural reform. we have always said (the commission has been saying it since at least since 2010) that we also need investment and to look at the social impact of the crisis. we need to keep up the investment measures necessary to make the reforms work to the benefit of jobs and growth. our number one aim is to restore employment: this is the biggest drama of today’s europe. we cannot say that we are out of the crisis while we keep these very high levels of unemployment; however, it is true that we have now exited a phase of the crisis where there were systemic threats to the euro area. this is why we need to resume investment in the economy. we still have a fragmented financial market where companies are paying interest rates not because of their intrinsic quality but because of the country in which they are located. this is an issue which i am sure deserves the attention of the european central bank. for terms of investment we need to use the european budget. for many of our countries and regions, the european budget is sometimes the only – if not the main – source of public investment. that is why we need to make the most of it. the european union budget is also an important tool for directing reforms and investments and leveraging loans for smes in close cooperation with the eib, as the european commission and the eib have proposed. we need to ensure that the new multiannual financial framework will deliver through the sectoral programmes, which will firm up the economic recovery. especially important is what the member states can do through the youth guarantee programme, now that they have adopted the youth guarantee. this programme was proposed by the commission and is now agreed. the focus now is on implementation. this has to be activated as a matter of urgency so that we can reduce youth unemployment; we cannot lose a whole generation. the greek presidency will also have other issues to discuss and in order to facilitate agreement in terms of the budget – for instance, on the own resources package and the start of the work of the inter-institutional high-level group on own resources created precisely between parliament, the council and the commission. it will be important, and will take effort, to deliver a balanced and successful european semester in the current context. the timing of the elections means that there will be very tight deadlines between the publication of the country specific recommendations by the commission and the european council at the end of june. we will need to work together in a constructive spirit to make sure the process runs as smoothly and transparently as possible, as was, by the way, the case last year. the greek presidency has rightly stressed the social dimension of economic governance, since no one knows better how important openness and legitimacy are to the eventual outcome of a process of profound reform. our focus on immediate measures to fuel growth and jobs does not in any way detract from the necessity of continued reform of the underlying european framework and our common economic policies. as i stressed yesterday in this house, completing the european banking union is not just one of our main deliverables but also our primary duty before the elections. parliament and the council now need to do everything they can to reach a final agreement on the single resolution mechanism. like you, the commission would have strongly preferred a system fully based on the community approach, and we have been defending this. as parliament and the commission, we should now work together to ensure that the intergovernmental dimension is kept to the absolute minimum of what is politically necessary, and we should be vigilant in ensuring that the outcome remains fully compatible with, and upholds the primacy of, european union law. the european commission will contribute actively to finding an agreement before the elections, as we believe this is indispensable not only for the banking union but also for deepening economic and monetary union. we need to remain active on all fronts until the very last day and adopt as much of our legislative agenda as possible, especially proposals such as those remaining under the single market acts i and ii and the telecoms package, which should provide economic opportunities for years to come; accelerate the economic momentum of today with investments for growth under our new multiannual financial framework; and stabilise our economic foundations through further adjustment and reforms in order to remedy the gaps and errors that have become apparent over the years. in fact, as the prime minister has said, there is good news: the important progress made in last night’s trilogue on the markets in financial instruments directive (mifid) is another step towards greater transparency and discipline in financial markets and another advance towards completing the comprehensive programme of financial reforms launched in 2009. at the same time, we need to continue working in areas that go beyond our efforts to remedy the economic crisis but are equally vital to demonstrate that the european union is capable of delivering the results our citizens expect: on data protection, which is so important for upholding our values and the way we want our society to function; on the rule of law, where this spring the commission will present a more robust mechanism to counter systemic threats that may arise in any one of our member states; and also on climate and energy, where the commission will come up with a concrete framework for the period up to 2030 and where we are determined to continue to take the lead globally. if you look at the new energy landscape emerging you will see that, for us to guarantee future growth and jobs, we will have to strike the delicate balance between sustainability, security of supply, and competitiveness. on these and other issues, the commission intends to work together with you, as long and as hard as possible, in order to get things done. with the date of what is possibly going to be a tense election coming closer each day, the political mindset in the months to come will often be unpredictable and political attitudes are in danger of becoming more extreme. we are seeing the rise of extremism from the extreme right and the extreme left. we must, however, make sure this campaign is an opportunity for more genuine debate about europe, not a festival of unfounded reproaches against europe. one issue in particular is already in danger of being subject to all kinds of populist rhetoric rather than objective assessment: the free movement of people in general, and of workers in particular, in the european union. the free movement of people is a fundamental principle of europe and of the treaties, and indeed one of the core elements that distinguish our union. free movement is an indispensable ingredient of european citizenship. it is one of the most popular fundamental freedoms with citizens across europe: when you ask people all over europe what is important about europe, the first thing they say is precisely that: free movement. it is also an indispensable pillar of the single market. without the free movement of people, it would not make sense to have a single market based only on the free movement of goods or capital. free movement contributes to economic growth and european competitiveness. let us be clear about free movement. the european commission as the guardian of the treaties will uphold this principle: it is a historic achievement, an advancement of civilisation, a true acquis of freedom, democracy and the rule of law. it cannot come as a surprise to anybody that the principle of free movement exists and is applicable throughout the union without discrimination, because we do not want a europe of first-class and second-class citizens. free movement is the result of decades of negotiations and agreements between the member states and also this parliament. it is in our law, and we should respect our common law. however, contrary to impressions created recently in national debates, it is not a freedom without rules. free movement is subject to european law, which has also been jointly decided by the member states in the council and by this parliament. these rules offer guarantees to citizens, and they also offer possibilities to the member states to deal with abuses. so, the member states are not only entitled, but also have the duty, to act if there is any abuse of this fundamental freedom. of course, as is the case in our democracies with national rules under national law, the implementation of european union law must be subject to appropriate procedures and, where necessary, to appropriate procedures of review and redress. this is why the commission supports national, regional and local authorities in managing this principle correctly, in particular in order to fight abuses when and where they arise. the commission published a communication last november even before these latest polemics and outlined five initial actions: (1) fighting marriages of convenience; (2) applying social security rules correctly; (3) making more use of the european social fund to promote social inclusion and fight poverty; (4) bringing local authorities together to help them learn from one another; and (5) assisting officials in correctly applying the rules on free movement on the ground. so let us work together in a constructive and objective fashion. let us not use stereotypes and myths. let us have a rational and reasonable debate. let us not give in to scaremongering and obfuscation. let us together preserve a precious good for the sake of all of us. it is good for europe’s competitiveness, europe’s markets, european citizens and all of us who enjoy freedom in europe and have an open idea of europe, and not a narrow chauvinistic idea of the protection of different countries. 2014 will see the first european elections since the entry into force of the lisbon provisions, which have so fundamentally changed the european institutions, not least in terms of the power and prestige of this parliament. 2014 will also be the year in which we commemorate the centenary of the first world war – a war which, as you know, started with events in sarajevo. yet, as the prime minister said, the countries of the balkans now see precisely in europe and the european union their hope for consolidating peace and democracy. this is a message we should be attentive to. from the european wars of the past 100 years to a european unification that has continued spectacularly with the enlargements of the last ten years, with all those memories in mind, i hope we will have a more profound european debate and that the european project will be more strongly defended than before. i hope mainly that mainstream political forces will sometimes be able to leave their comfort zones, and that they will no longer consider european unification to be given by implicit consent but will have the courage to explain rationally and with passion why we need a strong europe more than ever. whereas in the past, young europeans were killing each other in colossal wars, now – thanks to european integration – they will vote together to decide on their common future. what a difference! so let us make this vote for the european parliament another important moment to reinforce a true european democracy. by the way, these are two greek words: europe and democracy – another thing we owe to our greek ancestors. let us remember what is at stake, what the real foundations of european integration are, and what the dangers of disintegration could be. on that basis, let us work together to deliver the results that really matter with pragmatism; pragmatism, another greek word. i believe that, with this commitment to democracy and this pragmatic approach, the greek presidency will help make our europe stronger.
madam president, thank you, mr samaras, for your introductory words, but sometimes i have the feeling that you are a bit in denial. when i heard mr barroso, it was very clear that we all seem to be in denial. i will just refer to a number of data. if you just look at the recent gallup poll showing the approval of the eu leadership, which in greece was supported by 60 % in 2009, you will see that that declined to a level of 19 % in 2013. that is a huge decrease in the approval of the eu leadership. if we then look at the debt level of greece, we see that it is still at 180 %; unemployment is still rising in greece, standing at over 27 %, and youth unemployment is around 58 %. then, mr samaras, you say ‘we have been rebalancing our trade balance, we are back to zero’. yes, but how? by cutting consumption levels, that is how you are doing it. that is not the way. if we are not addressing all these kinds of issues, then what are we doing here? it was a kind of applauding machine at the beginning, but we have big problems, and i would expect greece to address them, because greece knows what mistakes we made. but what is greece doing? it is not welcoming the troika inquiry committee; it was not welcome last week, because it was not part of the party. also, there is no debate on a haircut on the bail-out loans, when we all know it is needed; and no debate on real, balanced social economic policies, instead of the austerity-driven obsession of right-wing politics. those are the issues to be addressed. and on climate, greece is still importing eur 9 billion of oil every year, so i would expect that greece to be a frontrunner in fighting for an ambitious climate and energy package. i also hope that mr barroso will do so, because up till now you have been getting compliments from tories like callanan. that shows how far down the wrong road you are heading now with climate and energy policies.
i would be grateful if mr samaras would listen and put his headphones on, because i have three tips for him. the question is whether his presidency will be an epic or a tragedy. i have three proposals for him to make this semester epic. first point, mr president. the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments. we see that the situation in the eurozone is still very uncertain. this is partly because of the skyrocketing debt of some countries, such as greece. yet he says: yes, the greek economy is going to grow. but what is the real situation in greece? what are the numbers? seventy percent of the greek middle class has disappeared. eight universities have closed in the last six months. cooking kitchens are in full swing. greece, mr president, is paying a very high price for remaining in the euro zone and the structural problems are not being remedied. because we see that the eurozone still does not meet even the basic conditions of an optimum currency area. one of those basic conditions is having exit criteria: the european council in brussels in december last year agreed on the way in which countries could exit the monetary area. and be aware, being outside the eurozone is not a punishment, quite the opposite! it is then that there is hope and that real opportunities arise, especially in combination with debt restructuring. then you can really fight economic malaise with monetary tailoring. so i would like to hear from the council president when he sets out those exit criteria, and how he is going to test them on greece. secondly, the negotiations on the new fisheries fund have stalled. as a result, dozens of dutch fishermen are not allowed to fish with the pulsars. these fishermen, who are innovators, have fallen victim to european bureaucracy. this is absurd! the president of the council must start these negotiations quickly and bring them to a successful conclusion. thirdly, unfortunately, the european union has been negotiating accession with turkey for many years. strange, because this country is mostly outside europe. the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force. the cyprus question is also at a standstill. my request to mr samaras: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. i would like to hear your reaction so that your presidency can truly be an epic.
madam president, first i too would like to congratulate prime minister samaras for the work he has done for the greek people and also for the european union. this experience should help make the greek presidency a success, but it will not be easy. we all want the banking union up and running as soon as possible. this is crucial in order to speed up economic recovery, but the council created major obstacles on the last legislative file in the single resolution mechanism. what started as a legal concern of one member state has now resulted in splitting the fund and in a complete overturn of the governance of the resolution mechanism into an overly complex system, leaving crucial decisions up to the member states. this will seriously undermine its effective functioning where bail-in needs to applied, because the banks have to pay themselves. it will also not assure equal treatment of banks, irrespective of the member states in which they operate, including equal treatment with regard to the resolution fund. moreover, because of that, it will not break the link between banks and sovereigns. following what president daul and others have said, this parliament does not trust banking resolution subject to the political bargaining of member states. therefore we cannot support a bad deal. prime minister samaras, you know the clear position of this parliament, and if you also want the file to be concluded before the european elections, you have to wake up the council. you have to get an ambitious mandate for the negotiation and you have to get it soon, in order to get it completed before the european elections.
mr president, first of all congratulations to the greek people on all that they have achieved so far. you still have a lot of work ahead of you, but the greeks will get through it. i will continue in english. the challenges for greece are not only in the area of getting the economy back on track but, as guy verhofstadt said, also in the area of cleaning up the political culture and ensuring that there is a free and pluralistic press, which is essential for a democracy. the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force. and what will it do about files which are stuck, mr samaras files on data protection, the horizontal anti-discrimination directive and the transparency regulation, which have been stuck for many months and years? finally, i would like to know whether the council can give some clarification on the state of play of the negotiations between the member states and the european commission and the united states concerning the foreign account tax compliance act (fatca).
the free movement of persons is a fundamental right, but all fundamental rights are subject to suspension, as the european commission has indicated in response to my questions. that is, when there is an emergency. the free movement of capital in cyprus has been stopped. if the collapse of a bank in cyprus is already an emergency, why is the out-of-control situation with internal mass migration not? millions of people move from country to country. the problem of housing, integration, training and the so-called 'benefit tourism' is everywhere. social systems will succumb to european rules if they grant rights where there are no duties. countries have been admitted to the european union that were not ready for it and that has led to the current problems. in the view of the european commission, it is high time that this situation was addressed by suspending the fundamental right of free movement. but to do so, the member states must take the initiative to protect their own social systems and their own people. and they have every right to do so, mr president.
two years ago, we had the first debate here on the malicious rhetoric surrounding free movement. the european parliament then took a hard line against prime minister rutte of the netherlands for refusing to condemn the stigmatizing poland checkpoint. rutte then promised to come here to debate with us. since then, it's actually only gotten worse. minister asscher is raising fears of a flood of romanians and bulgarians, when research shows that these fears are unfounded. secretary teeven is scaring people into thinking that social security is being abused, when research shows that fear is unfounded. i am already annoyed when opposition parties take a walk with the truth. but when government officials knowingly spread fear among their people with untruths, that is downright abhorrent. we still haven't seen prime minister rutte here. i call on him to keep his promises and to come to the european parliament at last, because, to my great regret, there is more than enough reason for this.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for her words and her proposal. it is good, but, frankly, not good enough. we have known for many years – way before the snowden revelations – that safe harbour was faulty and that it provided no more than a symbolic protection. it is not providing any protection for european citizens, but only legal coverage for companies processing data between the eu and the us in the knowledge that they are violating eu rules. because we did not want to make trouble for those companies caught between two conflicting jurisdictions, we have tolerated the situation for many years. we have asked the commission repeatedly, for many years, to come up with a solution, but the commission did not act. as a matter of fact, this famous evaluation of the safe harbour arrangement – because it is not actually an agreement – came, i think, two years after the deadline. so why now give more time to the commission and to the united states to come up with a solution? we know what the problem is. we know it does not function and, quite frankly, i am also losing my patience with companies. as i said, we have tolerated the situation for many years in order to give them a break, and what have they done in return? they have mounted a massive lobby in order to further water down data protection standards. as far as i am concerned, we should suspend the safe harbour agreement today. maybe that will create the necessary sense of urgency for the commission, the us administration and the companies to start working on a serious solution, because it is about time this house and the commission stood up for the rights and the protection of individual eu citizens. these should take precedence over international relations and even over business interests. it pains me to say this because i am a market liberal, but it is time that companies also took responsibility. in conclusion, i expect the commission to suspend safe harbour today and make sure that we get an adequate solution soon, before the elections.
we had a debate in this chamber earlier this afternoon on the free movement of persons. this is a very important point in the european union. a person who is a citizen of one of the member states of the european union can move and settle freely anywhere. that is something we must cherish in the european union. the right to grant citizenship is a prerogative of the member states themselves. no one will dispute that. the whole point is simply that malta has recently given - and other countries have given - a sobering interpretation of citizenship. i personally believe that citizenship should not be for sale. it must depend on what the personal relations of the person are with the country concerned, what the personal relations of the person concerned are with the citizens in that country. you can all make arguments, but there has to be a personal connection to that country. buying citizenship without ever having set foot in the country in question is, in my view, clearly wrong. malta is the most recent example. there are also examples from other countries and they can all be interpreted differently. i think that a clear distinction should be made between citizenship and residence permit in a particular country. there is much confusion about all this, but there is an increasing tendency to put certain privileges up for sale. the commission's reply, or the commissioner's introduction, was a strong response in my view. but i think it should be put on paper and there should be clear guidelines for the member states on what is acceptable in the european union and what is not. and it must be clear that buying citizenship cannot be acceptable.
mr president, it is becoming a ridiculous spectacle that the european parliament is meeting again on something that really has nothing to do with it. this parliament calls it a problem that countries use their sovereign right to issue passports for any reason. mr president, this is not an inverted world, but an inverted universe. the problem is the open borders and the terrible schengen agreement. without these regulations, this debate would have been unnecessary. this parliament sees a so-called problem which it cannot solve - fortunately - but does not look at the solutions it can propose. the next elections will therefore be about whether we want a federal state with a federal government, which can do that, or whether we accept that the treaty organization, the european union, is collapsing. i've noticed the latter for a long time. many dutch people are already completely done with the eu. the comments of mrs reding indicate that the euro-philists see a passport as an eu passport and not as an important sign of nationality. this elitist meddling will not stop. my party wants the netherlands to withdraw from the eu under article 50, and this debate only strengthens that case. "blue card" is the answer. it remains the prerogative of the member state itself to issue visas, residence permits and passports in any form. what you're dealing with, of course, are those golden visas from your government, and they're probably given to people who invest a lot. but let's be clear: if we had been talking about visas and passports for people coming here as refugees, or as asylum seekers, you would probably have reacted very differently.
nationality is for sale. residence permit for sale. do member states really want a situation where a rich russian can land his private jet to buy an eu passport and a boat refugee can arrive in his shaky boat and be allowed to return? how can we justify denying the free movement of persons to people who are entitled to it as eu citizens, while passports with price tags are sold at another counter? i'm really worried. of course, member states are themselves in charge of the access to their nationality. the only difference is that this has a direct impact on the rest of the member states. at the same time, it is also a ticket to other member states. that's what we agreed. obtaining citizenship should be based on objective, clear criteria, not on the thickness of your wallet. what will the council do to improve the arrangements so that member states can deal with this in a responsible manner? and how can the european commission help?
president. - i agree with mrs reding that citizenship should not be for sale. it's actually a very strange idea that you can buy passports. we as politicians must take nationality seriously and not play the populists again with this kind of action. passports are not for sale. and we all know - and i say this to mrs gomes - that there are of course countries which have investment schemes where you get a temporary residence permit. a lot of countries in the european union do that. but there is a fundamental difference between a temporary residence permit with conditions attached and the mere purchase of a passport, as is threatening to happen in malta. mr president, things have gone very wrong in malta. at first, we didn't know anything. it was classified, no public lists. now we know more about the conditions of the social democratic government. but the fact that you don't have to settle in malta is still a very strange thing, of course. you buy a passport in valletta and you settle in berlin or paris. of course, that has nothing to do with nationality. then, mr president, cooperation within the european union entails obligations. article 4 of the treaty states that member states should cooperate in a professional and confidential manner, i.e. not act as "einselgänger" on such sensitive issues. in conclusion: i agree with my colleague jan mulder of the alde group; i think it would be advisable for the commission to establish uniform, transparent conditions throughout europe for a temporary residence permit for investment purposes.

mr president, i am very happy that serbia is taking its next step now, and i hope serbian politicians will grasp this opportunity with both hands. reform can be such fun; it can be so good for everyone in society if properly implemented. serbia can take the opportunity to reform its judiciary, improving the life and rights of all involved: the judges, the prosecutors, the victims and the perpetrators. serbia can take the opportunity to improve its human rights, including everybody in society: lgbti people, women, minorities and roma people, thereby improving their economic growth, their employment prospects and their fight against poverty. it can even be good to keep visa-free travel, because eventually the only way to prevent ethnic minorities from asking for asylum is to give them a proper and good future within the country itself. serbia might take the opportunity to improve social dialogue, fighting corruption, fighting cartels and ensuring business independence, so that it will be fun to create and operate a business within the country. i do realise, however, that it is not only fun to reform. there are quite a lot of people – some with considerable power – who are profiting from the current status quo and do not want much reform. there will be resistance. i very much hope that the serbian government will face this resistance head-on and will overcome it. the best way to do that is to gather support from those who want the reform and who really want serbia to go into the future and into its european future.

mr president, this is only one hurdle towards much-needed broader, eu-wide copyright reform and the completion of our digital single market. our goal was to give artists more influence and choice over the rights to their works. we managed this by forcing cmos to be more transparent, by giving creators a stronger role in the governance of the organisations, and also by allowing for non-commercial licences, such as creative commons. it is essential that collective management organisations pay creators swiftly. the commission assessed the value of royalties at no less than eur 3.6 billion in 2010. cmos should do what they can to find the rights-holders and should be proactive in ensuring good governance throughout. the second part of the directive establishes the criteria a cmo has to fulfil to grant multi-territorial licences for musical works for online use. pan-european licences will now be available. for this house, measures to protect the position of smaller cmos and smaller language regions were essential in preserving our much-valued cultural diversity. these proposals are only part of what needs to happen to match copyright management with the opportunities that the digital era brings. although the commission is, sadly, dragging its feet, i am sure the call for reforms from our citizens will only get stronger. this new directive must not be used to further delay the modernisation and harmonisation of european copyright laws.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. - mr president, our citizens cannot understand why they are suffering from the effects of the economic crisis, while the criminals who helped to cause it are getting away with it. just look at the libor scandal. how many of those people are behind bars today? urgently needs a banking union to have a safe and stable financial system. we need better protection from failing banks for taxpayers’ money and better access to loans for our businesses and because the council’s position on the last building block of the banking union is creating major problems, putting the credibility of the single resolution mechanism at risk already in the first years. with the current slow pace of negotiations we might not get it right before the european elections. with the vote this week, parliament is putting pressure on the council to move substantially on key areas of concern. let me be clear: it could all be done under the current treaty, but i do understand to some extent the constitutional concerns of one member state with regard to paying bank levies into a european fund, that an intergovernmental treaty is needed for that. but what started as a constitutional concern over a specific issue has now resulted in a complete overhaul of the governance of the resolution mechanism. the system is overly complex, it leaves crucial decisions up to the member states and makes resolutions dependent on national resources. this will not work when a bank needs to be resolved over the weekend. therefore, substantial changes are needed because this could seriously undermine effective application of the resolution tools. we want to ensure that bail-in will be applied to make sure that shareholders and investors pay. but it should be equally applied to failing banks, irrespective of the member states in which they operate. there should be authority. we cannot afford to allow that to happen. the single fund has to be up and running from the start and this cannot be achieved by simply changing ten years into five years. the fund has to be operational in the first years too. we will be firm but constructive in the negotiations, because what is at stake is completing the banking union. but we have to get it right.
mr president, the financial transaction tax is a tax on investment which the nederlandsche bank has calculated will cost the dutch economy four billion a year. and the commission, too, is now admitting in answer to my questions that this tax affects dutch pensions, but also purchasing power and the economy. and then the plan is illegal. the transaction tax should not be introduced through enhanced cooperation, because it has an impact on the whole internal market. the proposal requires private companies to collect tax money for other countries which is illegal. and it affects the economies of countries that don't participate - whatever they're not allowed to do. in conclusion, mr president, it is an illegal plan that affects the citizens, the purchasing power, the economy and pensions. but fortunately, commissioner šemeta wants to introduce it now in small steps, because that will apparently make it less illegal and less harmful. mr. president, can we please be serious for a moment? a bad plan is not to be implemented slowly, it is to be thrown in the trash very quickly.
president. - we have two major problems to discuss today. the first is climate change, the second is the far too expensive and unreliable energy supply. now the great thing is that we can solve both of those problems with the same package of measures. that means that even those who still believe that climate change is a myth should support this package, precisely because it leads to a reliable energy supply and cheap energy. let me start with the climate, mr president. if we want to stay within the two-degree limit - and we all support this - we will have to reduce our co2 emissions by 80-95% by 2050. if you just go through what commissioner hedegaard said, 40% in 2030 is the absolute minimum to finally reach that target. i would like to see a target that is a little higher than the 40%, but i would rather have a realistic target that everyone believes in and wants to achieve than an unrealistic one. president. - the next item is the joint debate on: energy. without energy conservation and renewable energy, climate targets are unattainable. even without climate problems, these measures are necessary, because otherwise energy is far too expensive. look at the enormous cost of importing fossil fuels. more than 500 billion euros a year. every day we send 1.5 billion euros to countries like russia for our fossil fuel starvation. and that dependence is only growing. and don't believe in fairy tales like the shale gas. it is not for nothing that an oil company like shell does not want to invest in shale gas in europe. there's no future in that at all. so the only real solution to get affordable energy in europe is through renewable energy and energy conservation. and for that, binding targets are crucial. that has been proved in the recent period. finally, mr president, and commissioner oettinger also mentioned that if there is anything that industry, both the energy industry and the rest of industry, needs, it is certainty and clarity about the objectives and not 28 different national programmes, but a european, clear, binding narrative. "blue card" is the answer. mr president, mr krahmer would have been absolutely right if we had had a well-functioning ets system. but at the current price of 5 euros per tonne of co2, it is obviously not competitive with coal or other polluting, cheap energy sources. if we had had the co2 price of 30, 40, 50 euros per tonne, there would have been no need for a subsidy of euros for wind, land or solar energy. it would have paid for itself.

i would like to congratulate mr bach on the excellent result he has achieved. i am also pleased that an amendment by mr bach and myself has been included which focuses on dispute settlement. because a national enforcement body, as in the previous legislation, is only making a public statement and the consumer has nothing to do with it. he's got to take that to court. it is therefore important that there should also be independent dispute settlement which saves the consumer from going to court in an effective, fast and inexpensive way. the second point i would like to raise is this: we have had experience in the netherlands with this organ, but it has not worked because there is no level playing field. there should therefore also be an obligation for all carriers to become members of such a body. i am satisfied, mr president, and i hope that this will be maintained in the negotiations with the council.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations. we've already had the single permit directive. i don't know the dutch word for it. we have something called the 'blue card' and this commission proposal is a welcome addition to its completion. i think i have to compliment mr moraes on the way he has conducted the negotiations. with patience and persistence he has managed to get a large majority for this proposal today. several countries initially objected to the commission's proposal, the so-called 'yellow card' procedure. it wasn't enough to stop it. but now that i look at the result, i think a lot of those countries can be reassured. countries always remain sovereign in their decision of how many people they want to admit or not. there is no pressure from europe to let people in. it is still up to the member states to decide. in addition, there is a great deal of flexibility in the legislation as it stands. the period of work permitted varies from five to nine months. moreover, it does not have to be in the calendar year, but in a 12-month period. so i think there is a tremendous amount of flexibility for the countries, which they will welcome. this also applies to the issue of a visa or residence permit. that is for the member states to decide. i too think it is important - and mr moraes has also stressed it and other speakers have also said it - that we are talking here about european values in the field of labour law. social justice is an important point in our legislation. we must ensure that workers have good housing, that they have the same rights as national workers and that they are insured against health costs. again, the same rule applies here. there is no uniform european rule. the rules of the member state concerned are applicable. it is important that employment agencies can also make use of this. we have, as i said earlier, built a very great deal of flexibility into the legislation. more importantly, we make all kinds of rules, but those rules have to be enforced. that is only possible if there are also certain sanctions for non-compliance. i think that's well arranged, too. so i hope that this house will approve this proposal today by a large majority.
the fact that negotiations have been going on for three years shows how politically sensitive this issue is. migrant workers work all over europe and in these low-skilled jobs they often work without papers and without protection. but governments refused to touch it, and politicians chose to look the other way. and if you look the other way, exploitation continues. this law on seasonal work provides protection, that is, if the member states are also prepared to set up very good labour inspection here. otherwise some employers might be tempted to opt for the cheaper labour force, the undocumented labour force. for the greens, this piece of legislation - the first ever on labour migration for low-skilled work - also sets the stage for further debate on labour migration, not in seasonal work, but in other low-skilled occupations. i think of the filipino nannies, for example. these women work seven days a week in europe. their services are well-loved, but they live in a twilight zone, for they are practically all working here without papers, and therefore without protection. and that puts them at a huge risk of exploitation. europe is ageing if we do not legislate on labour migration. then we shall see an increase in the number of people working illegally. and that seems unwise to me for our societies.
today we are discussing a european directive on the management of the migration flows of seasonal workers. but time and again the european commission has made it clear to me that labour migration from outside the eu is entirely a matter for the member states. and yet this proposal. we must have a system of procedures for seasonal workers from third countries, we must have complaint centres and we must have social legislation. these are all areas that brussels does not concern. the european parliament now goes further: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. this is contrary to article 79 of the treaty which obliges the european commission to discourage illegal residence. the rapporteur's position is therefore incompatible with the treaty. it is therefore not only an illegal directive, it is also a redundant directive, because the member states are themselves legitimate, capable enough, and already have regulations for seasonal workers from third countries. it is clear that my party will vote against.
