member of the commission. - madam president, every year around 500 million tonnes of cargo are transported on inland waterways, particularly in europe’s most densely populated and congested areas. this silent and energy-efficient sector makes an important contribution to the european union’s transport system. however, the sector is undergoing difficult times and structural changes are needed to enable inland navigation to fulfil its full potential. in september 2013, the commission presented the naiades ii programme to improve the quality of the sector’s operating conditions and six key areas of intervention: infrastructure, environmental performance, markets, innovation, jobs and skills and the integration of the sector into the logistical chain. for each priority area, concrete initiatives are listed in the naiades ii communication. for instance, under infrastructure, light bridges will be adapted to today’s vessels, particularly container transport. the parallel technical standards of the different river organisations that generate costs for the sector will become streamlined across the european union. training and recognition of the professions will be harmonised and adapted to today’s demands and make the professions more attractive, just to give a few examples. what is more, the first steps towards implementation of these measures are already under way. the commission proposals for making available the reserve fund will provide much-needed financing for modernising vessels and upgrading professional qualifications. the proposal for technical requirements for inland waterway vessels will eliminate the fragmentation of technical standards for inland waterway vessels by creating a single set of standards across europe. both proposals are under debate in parliament and council. together with the rapporteur, the commission is hoping that they will be adopted as quickly as possible. let me take this opportunity to thank you, ms wortmann-kool, for your very ambitious schedule and the good cooperation on those files. for each of the six key areas mentioned, the commission has already, in its communication, specified operational outputs with target dates and identified the actors responsible for their implementation. it is regularly following up the implementation with all actors in the naiades implementation group. i will keep this house informed of progress. in that context it is important that the private sector fully assume its prime role for improving inland navigation. much more can be done by the sector to improve cooperation, share information and foster integration of inland waterway operators into logistic chains. we will of course support the sector in its endeavours. your second question asked how to stimulate innovation to increase the environmental performances of the inland waterway fleet and, in particular, to help the smes to access finance. the commission has programmed a financial contribution of eur 20 to 40 million for waterborne vessels for the period 2014-2015 in the horizon 2020 work programme. overall, eur 6.3 billion are available in the programme for transport and, of course, support for improving the environmental performance of the fleet will continue after 2015. the connecting europe facility (cef) contains eur 26.25 billion for transport to be used, inter alia, to remove bottlenecks and create missing links in the inland waterways network and provide financing for new technologies such as lng. the connecting europe facility instrument will be used in conjunction with the cohesion funds, under which another eur 6 billion is available for transport. the commission is also investigating how to facilitate access to support the inland waterways sector in its future investments and green technologies. helping the inland navigation sector also means strengthening its smes, which represent 80% of the companies in the sector. stronger and well-functioning associations and cooperation structures are needed to strengthen the position of these smes and to help them make better use of the available funding from the european union and member states. there too, the commission will provide support, for instance by proposing to open the reserve fund to fund associations representing the sector or by identifying the best practice in cooperation. to conclude, the commission has put in place an ambitious programme containing concrete measures with a lasting effect on the sector. naiades ii will be implemented with money from the european union budget but naiades ii can only succeed if all actors join forces. the commission therefore calls upon all stakeholders including the european parliament to take an active part in the implementation of naiades ii.
president. - the resolution that parliament has drawn up is quite clear. we have made it clear in the resolution that inland navigation is in trouble because of the economic crisis, the debts that many have. and then there's the huge overcapacity. our resolution also sends a very clear message. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, and i am sure that the commission will be able to take them up in the future. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. i regret that, because the present state of naiades is not adequate for inland navigation. the commission must really come forward with additional proposals in the coming years so that the sector can really be helped to move forward, also in a financial sense. the fine words of the white paper on transport must be translated into concrete action. at the same time, mr president, i would like to say: the sector itself is also on the move. more cooperation is necessary for survival. shipowners and organisations must join hands, stop bickering over competences and their own little kingdoms. if not, more and more sailors will be unemployed. the future of this wonderful sector looks bleak.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, we feel a strong sense of urgency to complete the banking union before the european elections. that is why we have to confirm today our strong and united mandate for the negotiations on the single resolution mechanism, a mandate that has already been voted through with a large majority by the competent parliamentary committee. this plenary vote should serve as a wake-up call for the council. it was good that the greek deputy prime minister was present at the debate on tuesday where this broad unity was confirmed. this underlined the great importance the greek presidency attaches to this regulation. he clearly indicated the willingness to work constructively on a common agreement, but the greek presidency cannot negotiate when they do not get a substantial mandate from the ecofin council and there is a clear urgency – the markets are watching, so we have to get it right from the start. when we negotiated the stability and growth pact, we corrected an institutional shortcoming. we moved the supervision on commonly-agreed rules away from governments, giving more objectivity. in the end we both agreed, and this is what made the ‘six-pack’ a credible and good deal. but now the council is suggesting the reverse: leaving crucial decisions on resolving failing banks up to the member states. it is hard to see how this would lead to effective and efficient objective decision-making, let alone how this would work when a bank needs to be resolved within 48 hours. it could also seriously undermine the effective application of the resolution tools. we want to ensure that bail-in will be applied to make sure that shareholders and investors have to pay, but it should be equally applied to failing banks irrespective of the member states in which they operate. equal treatment, where needed, with regard to access to the fund is another area in which the council’s position falls short, because the fund is rigidly broken down into national compartments. this could reinforce the damaging link between member states and banks, instead of breaking it. the risk of reputational damage for this new resolution authority is substantial, especially in the first years. we cannot afford to let that happen. the single fund has to be up and running from the start of the single resolution mechanism through a swift mutualisation of bank levies, instead of 10 years of national compartments. a lending facility is needed for that as well. the committee on economic and monetary affairs (econ) voted in favour of a clear position for a single resolution mechanism with a truly single fund, where a bank can be resolved within a weekend – a system where the banks have to pay. our group really wants to protect the taxpayers from having to pay the bill again for failing banks. the council introduced an intergovernmental agreement – a temporary one – but we have grave reservations about this, as we do not believe in intergovernmental solutions unless we are convinced that it is absolutely necessary in order to fulfil constitutional requirements, and also unless we are convinced that it is legal. i have some understanding for the constitutional concerns of one member state concerning the transfer of contributions from banks to a european fund, but we cannot accept an intergovernmental agreement that takes out other crucial elements of the single resolution mechanism and leaves them to the discretion of the member states. the negotiating table is here in this house – not in the council building. the trialogues with the greek presidency are the only format for negotiating for this parliament, and i would like to welcome the helpful contributions made by commissioner barnier during the debate on tuesday. his initial proposal for a single resolution mechanism is the right basis; it shows the right way. we also truly trust in his insistence on a credible and workable system. we trust that he shares our view that an agreement is urgently needed, but cannot be done at any price. completing the banking union is the number one priority for the ppe group in the last months of this term. the single resolution mechanism is the final brick we have to add in order to restore credibility, to ensure a safe and stable financial system with better access to loans for our businesses and households, and to protect our citizens and their deposits from failing banks. the banking union is crucial to regaining trust and speeding up economic recovery. the epp is aware of the great responsibility resting on us. we want a deal. we will now vote on this dossier to achieve one important purpose: to give a strong signal of unity. our line that was agreed in the economic and monetary committee will get the full backing of this house. we are not modifying our negotiated position, but repeating our views, which were expressed by econ already in december of last year. but we are not concluding our first reading; we are keeping the door open, and this should serve as a wake-up call for the council. parliament is ready and open to accelerating these negotiations and achieving a deal before the european elections. we will be firm, but constructive in the negotiations. however, we cannot just accept any old agreement. completion of the banking union is at stake, but we have to get it right.
i do not support the ferrera report. i am against the second pillar of the banking union: a fund that is gradually evolving from national compartments to a common fund to rescue banks. firstly, the fact that the liability for the debts of weak banks is being wiped out in one big pile in europe is problematic. the unknown bad loan bill is deposited indirectly with the european taxpayer and bank customer. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. the banking union is also a step towards political union. this centralization of power is a principle my party rejects. the second argument is that emu cannot work properly because competitiveness and public finances are too different. a source approach is required: reforms, austerity, write-offs of bad loans on bank balance sheets, partial cancellation of unsustainable public debt and exit from the eurozone where necessary. finally, it is inappropriate for the european parliament to complain about intergovernmental decision-making. it is more suited to economic policy than the community method, since the treaties only give the eu the power to coordinate economic policy. that is not an exclusive or even a shared competence!
author. - mr president, several non-democratic measures have been taken by the thai government and have led to massive protests in a generally peaceful country. the government is putting aside its senate, as well as the constitutional court’s rulings on the amnesty bill and on the date of the elections. on top of that, a non-elected people’s representation has been proposed. such actions have led to outcry and protest, and responses have led to death and injury. the prime minister has declared a state of emergency. these very troubling steps have paralysed the country and the elections, as voting did not happen in all areas of the country and the law forbids the opening of the legislature when it is not 95% filled. without a parliament, no new government can be formed. this deadlock must end, by investigations into the violence and the prosecution of those responsible. all parties must show restraint and respect for the rule of law and abide by democratic principles. this includes a government that ensures free assembly and free expression. it should start by lifting emergency laws. we welcome the fact that the military has remained neutral and we call on it to continue to do so. we also welcome initiatives of dialogue and reconciliation by all those who have contributed, and stand ready to play any role that may help to overcome the current difficulties and help thailand onto a democratic track.

the interference from brussels is becoming more extreme. the netherlands already had to submit its budget for approval, but the european union does not go far enough. now brussels also wants to control our socioeconomic policy. our employment policy, our tax system, our social policy, our pensions. brussels wants to have a hand in everything. it's called economic policy coordination in eu jargon, but it's your pure planned economy. the european commission's unelected bureaucrats oversee the member states. countries that listen to the brussels dictates get money. it all leads to more control, centralization and waste of money by brussels. president. - the european union is at an impasse. but you know, there is an alternative that is much better for the netherlands than all this meaningless planned economy, an exit. the netherlands is leaving the european union. research commissioned by the pvv shows that an exit is good for the netherlands. an exit creates growth and jobs, because the netherlands can again determine its own budget, its own trade policy and its own immigration policy. an exit means that the netherlands no longer has to pay billions to the eu budget, the bailouts and the emergency funds. an exit will ultimately bring the netherlands many more growth and jobs and instead of being dependent on brussels, an exit will finally make the netherlands a free and sovereign country again. the netherlands must therefore leave the european union. an exit, and as soon as possible!
we've already got the fourth rail package in front of us. the european commission wants to intervene in the way member states organise their rail transport. countries have designed their railways to suit their own needs and circumstances, and this must continue to be the case. it is therefore up to the member states to make their own judgments, for example on how efficient or user-friendly the railway system should be, or how innovative or, on the contrary, how frugal. international cooperation on the railway has been taking place for years. let it remain above all a matter of bilateral cooperation. we do not need the european commission for that. the commission's tactic is the usual one: it is the commission which is devising problems and then taking action against the member states. it gives its opinion on the way in which the member states have organised their rail transport. it then conjures up its mantra of market liberalisation as the solution to every evil. mr president, i should like to thank the commissioner for his reply. the eu is at a dead end. i would say: buy a one-way train ticket home!

i am sure that the system we are discussing today will help to achieve this, but i have two reservations. first of all, we must not pretend that this is a miracle cure. of course, very often the problem is not that there is no alarm, but that there are simply no ambulances available. according to a report yesterday, for example, there are calls in belgium, but the ambulances are simply not on time because they are too far away or because there is too much traffic. so there are all sorts of other factors that prevent emergency services from being on the scene in time, and not just because there's no automatic 911 call. i think that we should not pretend that we are giving a panacea for all traffic problems. if we really want to solve something, we might have to take a little harder action against drunk driving. secondly, we are dealing with extremely sensitive personal data. every time we make decisions like this, there's a reassuring saying: don't worry, it's only being used for this purpose, there's really no abuse, everything's taken care of. but we all know that in practice there is always abuse and there are always new and insidious uses for which the data are used. because let 's be honest . insurance companies, personal injury lawyers, police, intelligence agencies will no doubt find this information extremely useful. that is why my group has tabled amendments to allow citizens to decide for themselves. and if the system does turn out to be fantastic, it will sell itself! you don't have to force people, you can convince them! and so i would like to call on this house to support those amendments and for myself and my group that will be a red line to vote for or against the report.
the commission is also proposing that the european parliament should be given the right to vote on the proposals for directives. and yet there is a hint of this proposal. the presentations to the imco committee showed that in most member states the 112 hotlines are not yet prepared for this type of automatic assistance and will not be for the time being. the 112 services are still very much inadequate, as we heard from the commission recently. if practicability is a problem, it is natural to leave it to the member states to decide whether and, if so, when to introduce such a box. but no, we have to go to a unit sausage again. so this is not about saving people's lives. the german car industry is ahead of its competitors in the development of the cabinet and is interested in rapid introduction. insurance companies find it most useful to obtain more data on accidents. and there are many other commercial applications in the making. and yes, the police are also very interested in the spy box. with others, like sophia in the field, i've suggested that we at least build in an on-off switch, so that everyone can decide for themselves whether or not to use this. but even that proposal has so far failed. and so we have a system that is a direct attack on the privacy of the motorist and a plaything of the industry. there 's only one answer to this: rejection of the proposal.
this ecall file is bad news for the public. the restrictions on consumer choice and the violation of privacy are now a fact. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's transport policy is not disrupted. ecall is always online and transmits various data to a central hub. it happens in an accident, but it can happen outside the building. the device cannot be turned off. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. it's unclear who owns the data, and no one has ever considered whether the data can be used in lawsuits, let alone be used in court. we have not yet discussed undesirable matters such as the possible misuse of data and the possibility that this legislation explicitly allows for the equipment to be used for other purposes in the future. the pvv is against the compulsory installation of the ecall spy box in cars. let the control over the purchase of the system remain voluntary and where it belongs, with the consumer.

"blue card" question. what is it? mr van de camp, your party always advocates strict respect for the principle of subsidiarity. that means that the smallest possible entity has to make the decision, and that is the individual citizen. what i don't understand is: i understand why you want to introduce this system and perhaps it will indeed save a lot of lives, but i do not understand why your party wants to make it compulsory and not leave the choice to the citizen himself. what do we gain by europe forcing people to do what it wants? if it is a really good system, then the citizen can be persuaded and not forced to do so.
"blue card" is the answer. you may know that in my spare time i am a motorcycle rider. and a motorcyclist knows that accidents are always in the corner. you can't predict that. if at any given moment you are driving on a dark road in one of the new member states and you hit your car off the road, there is no choice of turning on or off. then you want to be saved!
traffic safety is not promoted by a chip in a car. people still drive too fast and still get behind the wheel with a drink. and unfortunately, there are also many places in europe where ambulances don't have a half-hour drive time, but take much longer. the introduction of ecall is a gift to the automotive industry. it's strange that you have to have a mandatory ecall, but you could have a commercial one. and you can't just go with "don't touch the chip". we can see what's going to happen here. mercedes is making a deal with an insurance company and bmw is making a deal with an auto repair company. meanwhile, data protection is highly questionable. okay, the chip won't do anything unless you put the car against a tree, but it'll pull gps. it 's only a matter of time before the police or the secret service think: this is a way for me to track people or until a hacker is smart enough to arrange to track you exactly where you are. the greens would like to spend the money that comes with this on road safety, but real road safety and not fake road safety like ecall.
the proposal for ecall is an unpleasant combination of wishful thinking and a curious lobbying by the automotive industry. this is wishful thinking, as the usefulness of introducing a mandatory ecall is questionable. international studies have shown different results. figures on the reduction in fatalities and serious injuries with ecall are not hard to come by. and effective relief depends on many other factors, such as the response time of emergency workers arriving at the scene of an accident. the fact that road safety organisations and car manufacturers are joining forces on ecall also suggests this. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal. because who dares to argue against road safety? if you take into account the considerable risks to privacy and the fact that real road safety depends on the driver's change of mentality, you will understand why i am against this proposal.
blue-card question. – i am basically going to put the same question to you that i put to our colleague, mr van de camp, and to which i received no answer. i am a bit confused here. all those parties that are always arguing against unnecessary interference by the european union are now calling for an obligation, something to be imposed on citizens. i am not arguing against the equal system. all i am saying is that the choice should be left to individual citizens. i would really like to understand why the ppe and the socialists want to put citizens under an obligation. what if i want to opt out as a citizen? why do you want to put me under an obligation? if the system is good, it will sell itself. i am leaving the choice to you, so why do you not leave the choice to me as well?
we are here again to discuss the so-called ecall system, a system which automatically transmits a signal to an alarm centre when there is an accident with the car. now, something has been agreed on this before, only it would have to be introduced voluntarily. but what happened? the public has no use for a snitch in your car who keeps telling them where you are. so the citizen justly left it lying around en masse. the commission has already done so. if the citizen does not want it voluntarily, we will simply make it compulsory. the euro-philists here are of course omnipotent and the citizens cannot think for themselves. but citizens can think very well and understand very well how the system works and just don't want it. what parliament is trying to do here, at least the commission and parliament, is pure elitist power politics towards the citizens. the data on the location of a car with such a system is always available. this is also shown by the fact that in the event of an accident the data can be transmitted with the direction of travel. there is therefore a continuous exchange of information between the cars and the security services. the citizen wants his privacy back and doesn't want the government to know where he or she is all the time. this system can never be so watertight that it cannot be misused or misused by the government. the argument that this will save lives, if it is made mandatory, is life threatening. i envision a next step where we just put a chip in everyone's head that alerts the security services if someone has a heart attack or stroke. then it really is helpful if they know where to go.

keep doing so, and i hope that her story will motivate you to do the same.
for the epp group i would like to follow the example of my dutch colleague, mrs de lange, and ask the council for its opinion on the situation of the severely weakened christian minority in iraq. the question i have to ask myself is: do christians in the two-stream country really have a future? i have heard from an insider, a local expert, that there are very pessimistic voices coming from amman yesterday. the outflow is continuous. demographic shifts are taking place where christians are still in the majority. the christian minority would be a plaything of rival shiite, sunni and kurdish forces. and also consider the proposal of the iraqi government for an autonomous region in the nineveh plain. in short, mr president of the council, i assume you are aware of these facts. i would like to know what the eu is doing to protect the iraqi christians? should they really be removed from the middle east, from iraq, from the legendary land of the two rivers? are we ignoring that? i like to hear what's going on on the ground.
the violence, the fear in iraq is immense. in january alone, 733 people were killed and 1,229 were injured. all because of terrorism and violence. the civilians in iraq are no longer certain of their lives. one hundred and forty thousand people have fled the current violence in ambar. terrorism must be stopped. but the people also feel let down by the government, especially by prime minister maliki, who has not fulfilled his promises to make real reforms and has failed to address the concerns of the sunni minority and other religious communities. when i look at a report by human rights watch and see that iraqi authorities are illegally detaining thousands of women, subjecting them to torture and threatening them with sexual abuse, i understand where that distrust comes from. that is why reforms must be implemented and maliki must ensure that those security forces do indeed guarantee the security of all citizens. we have made a great effort as the european union, alongside the un, to do something in this area. but i also see, when i look at the european union delegation, that not only does it have to work under very difficult conditions, but it also does not have the necessary material and resources to do anything. so i would like to ask you: do something about it! the unity of iraq must not be compromised; it is important to look at all vulnerable groups and religious groups in particular. my colleagues have already brought that up. there is an agreement between the eu and iraq and there are joint meetings to work on a commitment. there's an election coming up, but all that's not enough. i would like to call not only on political leaders but also on religious leaders to engage in a dialogue to see how this situation can be dealt with. what is happening now has absolutely disastrous consequences for the stability, not only of the country itself, but also for the whole region and therefore also for us. so i ask you: do something.
we are talking today about respect for fundamental rights. and every member state of the european union guarantees democracy, the rule of law and human rights. otherwise they would not be members. but in this parliament, president schulz is calling for the restriction of the freedom of the press by stating that a film critical of islam must not be distributed. this parliament wants to influence free discussions of free citizens by anonymous propaganda on the internet for a european project during the european elections. and this parliament still wants turkey to join, a country which does not recognise any fundamental rights and which imprisons journalists. mr president, this parliament does not protect fundamental rights at all, not even the right of peoples to self-determination, while the european union is developing into a superstate in which citizens are nothing more than the plaything of unelected bureaucrats. president, the netherlands must leave the union. my party is called the freedom party for a reason. the dutch flag must once again fly freely, and only then will the dutch people be guaranteed their fundamental rights.
the commission is also proposing that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. it can be an alternative to the european arrest warrant when a person is actually wanted for questioning and not for conviction. because when looking for evidence in a criminal case, the least invasive means must be used. the european investigation order is not simply based on mutual trust. the greens attach importance to the proportionality test which has been added and to the exception which can be made where fundamental rights are at stake. because trust is good, but control is better, and good example is good to follow. these two grounds on which the receiving party may refuse to execute the search warrant must, in our view, also be added to the european arrest warrant. the inability to get rid of the arrest warrant has led to untold suffering for european citizens in recent years, who have been transferred to another country on the basis of sloppy evidence and languished there in prolonged pre-trial detention under appalling conditions. i have repeatedly asked the commission and the council to do this and they have replied: this is collateral damage. - what about collateral damage? you tell that to a man or a woman who's wrongfully imprisoned and sees their job, their home, their relationship disappear? the fact that commissioner reding is now saying in her contribution that she would rather put off the changes for a long time shows that she does not understand the seriousness of the situation. the arrest warrant has now been in force for ten years without any proportionality test and without any account being taken of the inhumane conditions in prisons in some countries. we would like to see the rule of law functioning equally well in every country, but that is not the case at present and that is why mutual trust is not the right basis for judicial cooperation. because like i said, trust is good, but control is better. i therefore welcome the report by mr ludford, which calls for the arrest warrant to be amended. i expect you will. because getting arrested and deported for picking poppies on the side of the road or not submitting a video to the video store on time -- and i'm not making this up -- can't be a reason. mrs reding, i expect you to grant the wish we are putting forward here within a year.
in the debate on ukraine in strasbourg, hans van baalen, the shadow rapporteur for ukraine of the alde group, spoke out against the prospect of eu membership. article 49 of the lisbon treaty gives all european countries the right to apply for eu membership. van baalen, however, feels it unwise to speak specifically about this now, because it raises expectations which cannot be fulfilled. the vvd programme for the european elections on 22 may therefore states that eu membership for ukraine is not on the table. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. this cooperation is good for the european union, for the netherlands, which is one of the largest investors in ukraine, and for ukraine itself. during his visits to ukraine, the maidan movement and the opposition were concerned with democratic reforms in ukraine, fighting corruption and ending the oppression by the then regime. the ukrainians did not turn against russia and ask for eu membership. they called for democracy and respect for human and civil rights and cooperation with the eu.
i would like to thank mr hoyer, commissioner reding and our rapporteur, mr marinescu, for their contribution to this debate. reading the 2012 annual report, i was again impressed by the scale and also the results of the european investment bank. i guess you could say that. i think that few people know that the european investment bank invests twice as much as the world bank, for example. and it is therefore a crucial instrument for the european union. but despite this overall positive assessment, i also have a number of criticisms. first of all, i want to start with the lack of transparency. the european investment bank is the least transparent major international banking institution according to bankwatch, and i actually think that is unworthy of the european union. it is also regrettable because of the low visibility of the european investment bank. my second criticism concerns the choice of sectors. in 2012, some 30% of investments went to transport infrastructure. i think it is good and it is also in the operational plan 2014-2016 if the european investment bank is strongly focused on the future. the crisis has shown that europe needs to become even stronger for the transition to a smart and sustainable economy. and the european investment bank has a very important role to play in this. unfortunately, sustainability in particular is still a bit of a mess, even though that is where the future lies. in future, competition will be based much more on the efficiency of the use of raw materials, the reduction of harmful emissions and so on. i would like the european investment bank to put more emphasis on this, not only to save the climate, to save the earth, but above all to make the economic structure of europe much stronger.
mr president, as has been said before, the european investment bank has an enviable reputation: the bank has fewer staff than the world bank and lends more money. it can do that - and this is explained very well in the marinescu report - because it has a triple a rating. that is also an enviable position. this is partly because member states guarantee the capital of the european investment bank. my question, my general comment is: if those member states are gradually downgraded, if they also lose their triple a rating, what effect does that have on the lending capacity of the european investment bank? if we look at the recent multiannual budget of europe, it is lower than the previous ones for the first time in history. we automatically assumed that the european investment bank would be given more responsibility. a euro spent through the european budget is gone. a euro given to the european investment bank may be used 20 or 30 times. so that's much better on its own. we've come up with all kinds of new programs: eurobonds, transport bonds, more work for the european investment fund and so on. everything comes with more paperwork, more small projects and things like that. we have not had much experience with the european budget itself. the european court of auditors refuses to issue a statement of assurance for various parts of the european budget. how does the european investment bank intend to avoid its projects encountering the same difficulties as similar projects under the european budget? finally, mr president, there is talk of priorities, unemployment, energy, etc. i would say, in the light of the events in ukraine, let us concentrate on renewable energy sources.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, this report gives us an excellent opportunity to look back and to look ahead. looking back, i think the commission – as well as parliament – may be very proud of the directives that we have turned out, among other things, on maternity and paternity leave, and on women on boards. unfortunately, however, no-one has benefited from these directives in practice, because the council is not doing its part of the work. if the council is serious about gender equality, unblocking these directives needs to be a top priority. meanwhile, the commission need never be bored in the next term. parliament is eagerly awaiting new proposals for directives, such as on the gender pay gap and – a binding one, please – on violence against women, on paternity, adoption and filial leave. if all three institutions do their part, eu citizens will be able to notice a change for the better. finally, a short remark on the report itself: though we support almost everything that is in it, we cannot vote in favour if paragraph 67 on prostitution is not deleted. i sincerely hope that it will be.
rapporteur. - mr president, i am glad we have the opportunity for a brief exchange on the topic of transparency. i would like to start my intervention with a little quote from one of my favourite television series, called ‘yes, prime minister’, where sir humphrey appleby is saying to the prime minister: ‘open government, prime minister, freedom of information. we should always tell the press freely and frankly anything that they could easily find out some other way’. that is pretty much the attitude of the eu institutions today. colleagues, we all know that the trust of the citizens is at an all-time low. so this is the moment when we need to make eu institutions more transparent and give the citizens more trust. it is imperative that we develop a culture of transparency. for half a century, the european integration process has been driven by diplomats and civil servants – who did an excellent job, incidentally. but this is in the past. the diplomats have a culture of secrecy and discretion. they do not have a culture of transparency. today we are in a political union in a europe of citizens, and that means that transparency is a key element of the work that we are doing. before entering into the details of the report that has been adopted by the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs and that will be voted on tomorrow, i have just a few words on the epp alternative motion for a resolution, which surprises me because it seems not to enhance transparency but actually to reduce it. i find this surprising, because there are also members in the epp group who come from a tradition of transparency and openness. as a matter of fact, they are the very nationalities that have driven the process of transparency in the european union. if you read – i believe it is recital c in the epp resolution – they do not seem to have a very high opinion of citizens’ intelligence, because it reads ‘the legislative decision-making process is very complex, with very many preparatory, confidential and secret documents being vital for the whole legislative process’. that reads a bit like ‘do not worry your pretty little heads over it, citizens. we will sort it out behind closed doors’. and here i quote yet again ‘yes, prime minister’, where his assistant bernard says to sir humphrey appleby: ‘but surely the citizens of a democracy have a right to know’, and sir humphrey replies ‘no, they have a right to be ignorant. knowledge only means complicity in guilt. ignorance has a certain dignity’. now that may be fair for a comedy on television, but it is surely not fair for the way that we do politics in the european union. now, regarding the recommendations: first of all, of course we urgently need to revise regulation (ec) no 1049/2001, for which mr cashman here is rapporteur. this is really urgent, and it is unacceptable that the commission and the council keep blocking progress. secondly, transparency must be the rule and secrecy the exception. it seems that the practice today is the other way round. we have made a couple of very pragmatic and practical recommendations. for example, every eu institution, including this house, could appoint a transparency officer today. we have plenty of civil servants; we can appoint somebody to oversee the process, make recommendations and develop a culture of transparency. secondly, having extensive experience with litigation in court, i feel that we need lighter procedures to challenge negative decisions on requests for access to documents. it is absurd that we are burdening the courts with years and years of litigation. yes, president, i am coming to a conclusion. finally, we need better procedures for classifying documents and for requests for in-camera meetings. now it is a very random process which is left to high-ranking civil servants to decide. we want a regulation laying down criteria for the classification of documents and an independent oversight body to monitor that process.
blue card question. - first of all, let me say that i am actually not happy that mr cashman is leaving us after having done a great job for 15 years. secondly, i have spoken to the epp and have supported quite a few of the excellent amendments by your epp colleague ms corazza bildt. but my question to you is: do you not actually think that the citizens themselves are the best judges of what information they should and should not get? why is it that you refuse that kind of openness? it does not mean that everything is open; at the moment secrecy seems to be the rule. i do have experience in trilogues. i also have some experience in court, litigating for more transparency. do you have that experience, ms sommer?
- i'm the reporter. - mr. president, we are voting today on the fourth amendment to the law against money laundering and terrorist financing. and before i summarize the content, i would like to thank my co-correspondent krišjānis kariņš and the shadow reporters of libe, frank engel, emine bozkurt, graham watson, timothy kirkhope and dennis the younger. we have all made a sharp adjustment to the european commission's proposal on one crucial point. this parliament is adding a public register for final stakeholders. and with that, we're making it easier for banks and brokers to identify their clients. and that's what i'm concerned about as a green, and that's what i'm concerned about first and foremost, is that we make it a lot harder for criminals and tax evaders and tax avoiders to hide their money in letterbox companies in the european union. the fact that the son of the deposed ukrainian president yanukovych embezzles ukrainian government funds and deposits them in dutch trusts, as was reported in the news last week, makes me as a dutchman ashamed. last november, the dutch embassy in kiev held a meeting with tax advisers in which wealthy ukrainians were shown how they could safely and cheaply store their money in our country with the help of the dutch state. no one asked where all those millions came from. and did we all forget the definition of money laundering? money laundering is the transfer of money earned through criminal activities such as extortion and fraud into the official circuit. another example: gaddafi who put his oil dollars in a letterbox company in the netherlands. i'm not proud of it. tax evasion and tax avoidance are, in my view, serious crimes. how many roads are not paved? how many children in africa don't go to school? how many wounds are not healed when the coffers are empty, because the corporations put their profits into letterbox companies. thabo mbeki, former president of south africa and now chairman of the un commission investigating capital flight from africa, calculated that twice as much money is going out of africa each year as is coming in through development cooperation. misguided taxation leads to misguided development. a european public register for ultimate stakeholders makes corporate structures transparent and gives our society the opportunity to tackle immoral tax avoidance through openness and public pressure. it can set a trend in the world. let america take a good look at what we're doing here. prime minister cameron and president hollande have already advocated a public register. they see that in the end it is the citizens who pay for the benefits of the multinationals. angela merkel is against it and the dutch government, through mr. euro himself, mr. dijsselbloem, is also against it. he probably thinks the netherlands is going to miss opportunities and leaves the suspicion that he is continuing to facilitate tax evasion. just last week, minister dijsselbloem claimed that a public register could not be made because it would not improve privacy. well, i'll let him rest. what we are proposing in this european parliament is precisely what we are proposing in terms of data protection. there is nothing in that register but what is strictly necessary, and we have also given the possibility to bank customers who are rejected in some way to appeal without giving them their details, because we do not want to give criminals any insight into their situation. this parliament is ready to negotiate with the council, but unfortunately the period is running out. we are voting on this today, but we invite the council which is not here today to sit down with us.
mr[nbsp ]president, why should it be a secret that someone owns a part of a certain company? it seems only logical to want to know who you are doing business with, or to know that the business you are doing is legitimate. hiding company owners and using obscure corporate constructions to avoid detection by the authorities are tactics used by criminal networks to launder money. citizens are looking to us to take action. to quote one of many (a dutch lady), ‘the european parliament can stop criminals hiding money and robbing poor countries.’ so please help to reveal who is behind european companies and trusts. criminals use such networks and operate on an increasingly international level, further complicating transparency of ownership. we want to prevent drug lords, people[nbsp ]traffickers, gun[nbsp ]runners and corrupt businesses from setting up companies to launder their dirty money. in order to tie criminal activity to its perpetrators and facilitate prosecutions, we must be able to uncover who owns a given company. the rapporteurs, judith sargentini and krišjānis kariņš, did not initially propose the full openness of registers. for us, the social democrats, full openness was a key priority. without that, we would not have supported the proposal for a directive. we want a public register in full compliance with eu data protection rules. therefore i am glad that, in the end, parliament is supporting that approach. another important element put forward by our group and adopted in committee concerns better witness protection. whistle-blowers who are brave enough to share information in order to dismantle criminal networks should have the protection they deserve.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i am actually a little surprised by this, because i have received signals from the european commission that the commission was indeed interested in a public register. let 's be honest here . the proposal you had put forward was simply not good enough. you suggested that a company should know who its ultimate stakeholder is. the banks said, if that's the case and we go in and inquire with that company, we'll immediately send a signal that they should put their ultimate stakeholder a little deeper. the same goes for your story about balancing data protection and privacy versus effective tracking. you had no data protection in your proposal at all, with the consequence that the industry said: we are being squeezed between two legislations. how should we view anti-money laundering legislation in the light of data protection legislation? this parliament has devised a solution to this problem, which i believe is very effective. a public registry leads to a self-cleaning society. because let's be honest here. it wasn't that those dutch trust offices that facilitated those millions of ukrainians didn't think: that might be a little dodgy business. they could have felt on their dutch tongues that this money was coming from the wrong activity. and they went for it because they thought it was actually worth it. a public register gives journalists the opportunity to investigate. that's what we're looking for, because we want our society to clean itself.
i would like to thank the president-in-office for his kind introduction. yesterday we had a long discussion in this house about the role of the european investment bank. the situation in europe is that for the first time we have a multiannual budget for the next seven years which is lower than the previous multiannual budget. this means that the role of the european investment bank will be considerably increased. the philosophy is - and this has been demonstrated in practice in various circumstances - that if you spend one euro from the european budget, that euro is gone. if you give the same euro as guarantee capital to the european investment bank, the consequence is that it is used 20 or 30 times. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that's an excellent case. that is another reason why this report is a good development. we are increasing the guarantee capital of the european investment bank and that means that the european investment bank can lend more money. because the european investment bank is a special bank. the guarantees are given by the public authorities and it is only because the european investment bank can borrow cheaply and lend at a lower interest rate. we must maintain this role and we believe that this report can make an important contribution to that end. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the developments in ukraine make us realize once again that it is essential that europe does not give the impression that it is abandoning these countries at this time. this report can help to do that.

- i'm the reporter. - thank you, mr president, thank you, commission, and fellow negotiators and other members, thank you for being here for a final debate on f-gases, fluorinated gases. now, that sounds technical, but they have a huge impact on our climate. first, a little bit of historical perspective. we started in the 1980s with the montreal protocol to phase out cfcs and hcfcs. they were responsible for the hole in the ozone layer. it's a very successful protocol to phase out those f gases, the cfcs and hcfcs, but it's been replaced by perfluorocarbons and gases like sf6. its use has increased by 60% in the eu since 1990. so that's a huge increase in these f-gases, which have a very large greenhouse effect. they have no effect on the ozone hole, but they do have a large warming potential in the atmosphere and are very important greenhouse gases, sometimes up to 23,000 times as strong as, for example, co2. we have received the commission's proposal to phase out these f-gases, partly because it is necessary. if you're going to look at what the eu needs to do in the longer term to phase out greenhouse gases to a level of -95% after 2050, you also need to do something about the non-co2 greenhouse gases like the f-gases. the f-gases are a good example of the green economy. we have seen during the negotiations that there is an industry which has long been committed to alternatives. think of alternatives in refrigerators, air conditioning, insulation foams, and so on. for all those kinds of applications where there are f-gases, there are companies that say, we have alternatives available and we can deliver now. they therefore also called for stricter eu-level objectives and stricter legislation. on the other side, you have industries that have invested in f-gases and have, frankly, just been asleep. they lobbied very hard, of course, for no legislation, no tightening of the legislation. that was the battle within the european parliament, and also within the commission or the council: what industry you choose. for the new innovative ones and in this case also for the small and mediumsized enterprises within europe, because that is where the innovation is or do you opt for the large companies that are only stuck in f-gas production and very often also come from outside europe? i must say that i have been able to negotiate very well in parliament with my colleagues, such as jo leinen and mr bánki. we have reached a very good compromise in parliament. then we had to negotiate with the council and it is thanks to the lithuanian presidency that at least a deal was reached at the end of december because it was very difficult to convince the member states - as is often the case with climate legislation - of these opportunities for the green economy. the council was really the problem as is often the case with legislation. i thank the lithuanian presidency for the pressure it has exerted to reach an agreement. the agreement actually preserves the essence of the commission's proposal, i.e. a total phasing-out of the programme to -79% by 2030. but we have also been able to gain some ground as a parliament, such as additional prohibitions for specific sectors, think of commercial refrigeration systems, think of air conditioning with the highest greenhouse gases. we didn't get all of it. unfortunately, we have had to give up some of the f-gases levy, but fortunately we have a review for 2017 and i hope the commission will make good use of that too. finally, i would like to say that europe has once again been able to play a leading role in this matter. our agreement even led to an editorial comment from the new york times. and recently, california has indicated that it wants to legislate along the lines of the european union. so we can say we're in the lead here once again. with one small caveat: in california, they're gonna put a tax on the f-gases. let's bring that up for the 2017 review, because actually everybody's already doing it where it's successful. so why shouldn't the eu do that? but perhaps that is more for commissioner connie hedegaard, to whom i would like to thank in conclusion, and in particular her officials who have made it possible for us to reach this good compromise.
the rapporteur. - mr president, i will be brief, because we must also enjoy the moment when a subject such as climate does not, for once, cause a great deal of controversy in this chamber. so that is something to celebrate, especially because i think we all see that these very proposals are a backbone of the green, innovative industry. you can create new jobs, you can combat climate change and you can therefore support the innovative industry of a new economy in which europe can play a leading role in the world. this is a good time to make that a reality, and this legislation will certainly do that. it is also important that we set a very clear precedent internationally - and i would like to join commissioner hedegaard in this. the united states and china indicated last june that they want to take the lead on climate change and explicitly said that f-gases are a good topic for cooperation. i am very pleased that the eu is now setting the bar high for the united states and china to actually work together on this front and follow the european example. and so we're going to phase out these f-gases globally and drive the new economy that's innovating for alternatives. we're going to phase out the f-gases once and for all, at the right pace, but ultimately with a clear goal: we're going to get rid of the f-gases and we're going to get into the green industry. i thank everyone for that, including the commissioner and mrs auconie. if it's all right with you, i'd be happy to join you for a round of champagne. rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality and on behalf of the verts/ale group. - mr president, i would like to thank the shadows, and mainly ms[nbsp ]bauer and ms[nbsp ]berès. they did exceptionally well in the trilogue. i think ms[nbsp ]b and the other ms[nbsp ]b did a really good ‘good cop, bad copʼ impression that worked wonders, and i must also admit that both the commission and the council were quite helpful as well, especially compared to some other trilogues. the end result is a very consensual and very good directive. this directive is not going to rock the world, but it will benefit eu workers in several ways on the ground, and that is where it matters, because having rights does not mean that people can actually live out their rights, and i think we have made progress towards that. people will have more access to the information they need to exercise their rights. they will have more access to judicial procedures and also to group claims. they will have more access to equality bodies, and these equality bodies need to be properly funded to execute these tasks. we as greens are particularly happy with a prohibition on victimisation being included, because everybody needs to be able to claim their rights without being victimised for it. we are also very happy with the inclusion of rights for family members, including mutual recognition, so that a same-sex couple from the netherlands going to italy does not suddenly find out that they are not married any more according to the member state that they are working in. it is unfortunate that many people – many populist groups mainly – do not understand that problems with replacement and exploitation and the other problems connected to free movement can only really be combated by ensuring workers’ rights, and i think that this directive has been a good step in that direction.

in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken the initiative in the form of a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. the aim of ensuring that there is good cooperation in order to reduce the number of flights and thus reduce travel time and fuel consumption was of added value, not only for consumers but also for airlines. this was the intention of the ses2 package. the ses2+ package is, however, of a different order. the european commission, through this proposal, intends to intervene in the way in which countries have organised their aviation and the associated services (e.g. air traffic control, meteorology, etc.) and wants to require that these services be contracted, scaled up and organised in accordance with ec requirements. the pvv considers this interference unnecessary and undesirable. the existing single european sky package is sufficient for the objective of efficiency in air traffic, the new package adds undesirable elements that infringe national control.

member of the commission. - mr president, first of all may i sincerely thank the members of the committee on agriculture and rural development and the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. i would particularly like to thank the three rapporteurs, mr pirillo, ms paulsen and mr fajmon, and their shadow rapporteurs for their hard work in compiling the reports before us today. i believe that these reports form a solid basis for future progress, both in the council and under the next parliament. we will not agree on everything, of course, but there are a lot of points where both council and parliament could agree, with the help of the commission. let me comment briefly on each of the three elements in turn, starting with official controls because this has been mentioned by a number of meps. the proposed new rules on official controls seek to help member state authorities to deliver their control and enforcement. it is useless to have a law if they do not have the means – financial or human resources – to enforce it. so consumers and honest businesses should be reassured that control efforts meet two objectives, namely safety along the agri-food chain and the countering of possible fraudulent practices. in order to achieve this, we need an adequate level of funding. funding for these official controls is essential. therefore, it is true that the proposal provides for mandatory fees that would enable the recovery of the costs – not more – incurred by the competent authorities in charge of controls. you will be aware of some public spending reviews, which are being carried out in the member states and which are affecting the number of controls performed by national authorities across the food chain. this reduction can have potentially devastating consequences. the horsemeat scandal – which was never a food safety issue but a serious issue of fraudulent labelling – reminds us of what might happen if we lower our guard and do not have enough controls. therefore, the sustainability and credibility of the control system as a whole is at stake. when fees are necessary – as in this case – it is the duty of the regulator to ensure that they are applied in a fair, equitable and transparent manner. to achieve this, we need to have clear and uniform methods for the calculation and application of fees, and stringent transparency requirements. we also need to ask all sectors to contribute, in contrast to the current system where only some businesses are charged. however, micro-enterprises across all areas will be exempted. this means that 80% of all operators will not need to pay fees. in addition, the other 20% will be inspected on the basis of risk. this will allow for better prioritisation of controls and a more efficient allocation of resources. so an enterprise which is law-abiding would face fewer controls and therefore fewer expenses. the proposal also provides for improved tools for cross-border cooperation between competent authorities. this will ensure that cases of non-compliance which have effects in more than one member state – as usually happens in a single market – are actively and effectively remedied. the mechanisms for cooperation in the area of food fraud that the commission has been piloting in recent months will be strengthened under the new regulation. one of the proposals is that, when we have a scandal like the fraudulent labelling of horsemeat, the pecuniary penalty imposed on the company breaching the law must be at least equal to the illicit gain made from the fraudulent labelling. finally, consumers and importers of animals and products will benefit from a modernised border control system. what does this mean? it means documentary and identity checks on all consignments, physical checks based on risk, simpler procedures and full use of electronic documents and certificates. this is the border control system as suggested in this proposal. turning now from official controls to animal health, our main objective is to introduce a single piece of legislation to regulate animal health in the eu. we need a simplified system, because in this way we can better detect and control animal diseases in the framework of the farm-to-fork approach to food chain safety. i am aware that the number of commission empowerments in the proposal has raised some concerns. however, i would like to remind the members of parliament in this house that our proposal brings together 37 different directives and regulations. these currently contain 600 empowerments. we are now going to reduce these empowerments to 163. we also need flexibility in our animal health legislation. the commission’s proposal aims to strike the right balance between due deliberation and swift action in response to emergencies. in particular, i note that some wish to see the list of diseases relating to animal health and species adopted in an annex to the regulation. the commission maintains that the adoption of this list through implementing acts is legally sound and is practically and technically necessary. after all, this is not a new approach. it is in line with much of the current animal health legislation, such as the lists of diseases affecting aquatic animals and requiring union notification. if a new disease were to emerge, for example one similar to schmallenberg but with more serious effects, the commission would have to take swift action to combat it. therefore, the commission needs the flexibility to easily amend or adapt rules in response to the development of the disease. as i said at the beginning of my address, i welcome many of the amendments in the report by the committee on agriculture and rural development which are constructive and within the spirit of the proposal. however, i would caution against pressing for amendments that could perhaps go against our common goals. for example, there are amendments being proposed that would allow member states to restrict the movements of animals or products within the eu if they themselves judge there is an animal health risk. this would be a step backwards, because it risks undermining our existing systems and the internal market. there are certain amendments – as ms paulsen said – on animal welfare and antimicrobial resistance. we recognise that these are important. animal welfare and amr are becoming increasingly present in political agendas, and this is extremely positive. this is why the commission believes that they need to be considered separately and specifically. the commission is already taking steps to improve the implementation of what we already have in the current animal welfare rules and of what we have already achieved, particularly for laying hens and pregnant sows. as regards amr, let me assure you that we will continue to deliver on our action plan in this year. finally, regarding plant health, the proposal on plant pests would introduce important new elements for phytosanitary protection and the sectors concerned. these include regulating pests on the basis of established criteria for risk assessment and management, prioritising those pests with the most serious consequences, general measures for eradication, surveys and notification systems to ensure early action, uniform plant passports to certify the health of plants – so not only horses have passports, but also plants – and principles for risk assessment and risk management. i should stress the importance of the import rules set out in the proposal. these offer the possibility to prohibit or restrict pests, through appropriate measures, in response to identified and well-assessed risks. they would also allow the provisional suspension of imports of plants posing hitherto unidentified risks. this precautionary approach would strengthen the current import requirements, ensure preventive action wherever necessary, and support our importers in satisfying the considerable eu market demand for plant products from outside the eu under safe conditions. we also need to adapt our measures to a dynamically changing environment of pests and trade. therefore, our import strategy must be easily adaptable to scientific and technical developments. let me recall the following examples: the western corn rootworm, which was introduced into the eu in the 1990s through material of non-eu origin; the red palm weevil, an insect which arrived with palm trees from egypt and was notified by spain in 1996; the pinewood nematode, which was first found in the late 1990s in portugal and comes from north america or china; longhorn beetles – damaging for broad-leaved trees – which are regularly detected in wood packaging material from china; and the xylella, a newly detected bacterium in southern italy, in the region of apulia, which is now attacking many olive trees. in many cases, owing to insufficient controls and/or untimely reactions – and despite subsequent efforts and budget allocations – all these pests have proved impossible to eradicate. therefore a pro-active approach is necessary – sorry if that is a cliché, but i think it is the only appropriate word – combining the strengthening of the import regime with swift detection and eradication measures. many of these new elements will be accompanied by financial incentives through the regulation for the management of expenditures, to which i am glad the european parliament has already given its agreement. the union will co-finance costs linked to the early detection of new pests through survey programmes. it will also co-finance costs linked to eradication measures, including through compensating owners for the value of their destroyed plants. i look forward to a fruitful debate.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. - the envi committee takes this animal health law very seriously. i would like to thank my colleagues and the shadow rapporteurs of the envi committee very much for the good cooperation. it is a pity that a significant part of the opinion of the envi committee has not been taken up by the agri committee, because there is a really clear link between animal welfare and animal health. this report is the best place to express that. i believe in the principle that prevention is better than cure. why unnecessarily remove so many animals in the event of an outbreak of animal disease and put farmers in a very big predicament when you can prevent such situations with preventive policies? for example, by discouraging animal hoarding in areas or by rewarding farmers who give more space to their animals and not forcing them into intensive livestock farming. but also by limiting the endless transport of animals. this will help us to reduce the risk of unnecessary spread of disease. mass evacuation should only be a last resort. that does not mean that we should just give all animals preventive medication. because the problem of antimicrobial resistance is lurking and responsible policy is therefore needed, and very quickly. finally, i would also like to say that this legislation is ideal for building in traceability of cloned animals and their offspring. ninety percent of clones are stillborn, and the rest are largely diseased. so this is a subject that does indeed belong in animal health legislation. at the same time, consumers can be informed about the cloned product on their plate. two birds with one stone, that seems to me to be very efficient.
i shall speak on the report by my honourable colleague mr fajmon on plant health. of course, the right measures against disease are needed. however, amendments have been adopted in the committee on agriculture and rural development which go far too far. the plant sector has sounded the alarm. people fear rules that are unworkable. a closed system is not the answer. the plant trade is coming to a standstill. it means high costs and long procedures for the plant sector, even when there is no chance of disease. so that's what we call in the netherlands "shooting a mosquito with a cannon". what's the way forward? the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. we must keep trade open. separate votes have been requested on these amendments by the epp, the ecr and the liberals. - very good. ladies and gentlemen, let us all work together to remove these protectionist elements from the report. that gives room to the plant sector in europe and also gives room to developing countries which export a lot of plants to the european union. i am counting on common sense in the vote on this tomorrow.
first of all, i wish mr dehaene a speedy recovery. fortunately, he has found a very capable substitute to speak for him tonight. as regards the outcome of the negotiations, i would point out that we negotiated in the midst of a very serious economic crisis and that we agreed to a budget reduction. this is quite unique, because despite the austerity measures in the member states, budgets are still rising in most of them. so as far as i am concerned, the european parliament has set the right example. i think the main problem is that the problem of own resources has not been solved and we have set up a committee to deal with it. if the budgetary authority makes certain commitments, it is very difficult if one part of that budgetary authority then says: we have made the commitments, but we have no money to pay. so you have to reduce the budget for payments. that is a system that is hardly working and i hope the commission will find a solution. finally, i believe that a five-year period is ideal for a multiannual budget.
today is a historic day in the history of economic and monetary union. the agreement on the european banking resolution authority is the final building block of the banking union. in addition to the ecb's supervision of our banks, problem banks can now be dealt with at european level. that is crucial for restoring confidence and also for restoring the important role that banks play for our smes and for our economy. the position adopted by the council of ministers in december was inadequate on crucial points. the voting proportions would favour a few large member states in the first years and this political interference threatens to undermine the strict and equal application of the rules for the restructuring of failing banks. the agreement will ensure that it is not the taxpayer but the shareholders and investors who pay for the failing banks. this is true not only of portugal and ireland but also of french and german banks. this will encourage shareholders to focus more on managing risk responsibly than on maximising short-term profits. i would like to thank my colleagues, but also michel barnier, the presidency and eurogroup president jeroen dijsselbloem, for making this agreement possible. this is my last speech as a member of the european parliament and vice-president of our group. i am proud of what we have achieved in overcoming the euro crisis: the commission has also proposed a number of measures to improve the economic governance, six-pack, two-pack, aimed at responsible fiscal policy and strengthening the competitiveness of member states, financial sector legislation and, today, the final piece, the banking union. we are on the right track, confidence in the euro and the eurozone countries is returning. the economy is recovering, but unemployment is still unacceptably high. it takes time, but here too the trend is turning in the right direction. finally, the point is that we in europe and in the member states deliver what we have agreed. this is crucial for our society, because it leads to the recovery of smes and also to jobs for our citizens and in particular for our youth, and that is what matters.
- i'm the reporter. - may i say that i am proud that after almost ten years, we have now settled this matter once and for all? pensions are of great importance to the ageing and longer-living population in europe. if you are punished for working in another country, it does not help freedom of movement. we are already seeing more and more young people studying in one country and working in another. this proposal should ensure that there are no disadvantages but also benefits in taking advantage of the free movement. the proposal now before us on the portability of accumulated pension rights is in fact a continuation of what we already have in the 1408 coordination regulation. pillar 1 pensions are always paid for the years of work in another member state. that was not quite the case for a second pillar pension, the supplementary pensions that you often build up in business. now we give this member of a pension fund the opportunity to join earlier: as a german working in luxembourg, you can start to build up a pension at the age of 21. and secondly, if you've worked for an employer for three years, you're also sure that this pension won't be lost. this proposal is in line with the principle of free movement. we also think it is very important that someone with a dormant pension should be treated in the same way as someone who already has a pension and thus be indexed, because dormant pensions are usually not beneficial. but this condition is now part of the package. i have another comment on the question of equal treatment. people who use the freedom of movement are treated better in some member states than a worker who moves from one company to another within a country, for example germany. a number of member states must also take action on this point. we have a good proposal here. i thank the colleagues who have worked on this and it is not just the colleagues who have worked on this in the last six months. all those who have preceded us in
