on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, once again we are discussing here a new crisis of confidence. i think it is very urgent that the dark clouds that we have all observed are lifted. my group leader has rightly said that the united states, since the last elections, is a lame duck, and the world cannot afford a second world power which is completely paralysed by another crisis. so the eu has to act swiftly. there has been a lot of finger-pointing this afternoon, but let us just recognise that we all have a shared responsibility; the commission, the european parliament as well as the member states. i would like to say that i see eurosceptics of the left and the right are smelling blood, but all they propose is to stick to the status quo. i would like to know from the commission exactly what the investigation entails, other than the four cases of potential illegal state aid. what about the other hundreds of agreements that were exposed by luxleaks? incidentally, i would propose that the special committee for an investigation, that we suggested be set up in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, looks into that matter. i heard from commissioner moscovici, who is representing the commission in this case, about future legislation aiming in particular to eliminate tax avoidance. we would also like to know about past cases that are being investigated, and i would like to have seen here our liberal commissioner ms vestager whom, incidentally, i trust 100% to lead the investigation. (the speaker agree to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer. – speaking of missing the point, mr etheridge, i did not compare the tax system of the united states to that of europe – not remotely. as a matter of fact, we do not even have a common tax system. all i was saying is that the us administration at the moment is a lame duck as the result of the elections, putting the administration opposite congress. i am afraid that the european union will also become an administrative lame duck because there are these dark clouds hanging over the administration. that is what i was comparing, mr etheridge.
madam president, combating tax evasion, tax fraud and tax havens is a global, broad and complicated issue. there is nothing wrong as such with competing on investment climates and business climates, but this also has a state aid aspect. in this area the eu has an important role. we think it is a good idea to install a specific committee in this parliament to have a debate on these issues. however, the focus should be on principles not on specific cases or countries. on the global level the oecd has done some good work with its recommendations and we advise that we use this as a starting point.
of course, tax evasion must be tackled, because it cannot be that ordinary people and smes pay full tax and multinationals do not. the commission's choice of state aid is a mere means. the commission requires member states to provide information on tax arrangements with companies. it then assesses whether there is any unlawful state aid. the commission shall take these decisions in complete independence. neither the national parliaments nor the european parliament can influence this. in fact, they do not even have the underlying information on those tax agreements. that's the world upside down. the member states are already working together in the framework of the oecd in paris. why does the commission not make efforts to facilitate this process? the fact is that the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european parliament is not a mere spectator. it is more democratic and ultimately more effective, because it is based on support rather than dictation.
the practices we are discussing today are not new, as we all know. they're not illegal either. but not everything that is legal is desirable. i think that's the heart of this debate. let us admit that the tax rulings we are talking about rightly confirm the feeling of dishonesty of citizens and small businesses who pay their taxes properly. of course, there is nothing wrong with healthy competition per se, but there is something wrong with it when it leads to discrimination, state aid or unfair competition. then you must act. and if we are to take real steps against tax evasion, then we as the european parliament will need this european commission very badly. the council is not enthusiastic about this and it is decided by unanimity. but be careful. we also know that some in this house are completely unable to tackle tax evasion. some people just automatically put their heels in the sand. there are also those who want to change the vote on the juncker commission because he was not their candidate. and there are others who see this debate as an excuse to push the overall level of taxation in europe even higher. that is not our agenda as the ppe group. our question to them is: can they leap over their own shadow and let go of their personal vendetta or their secret agenda to tackle collective tax avoidance? our hand is outstretched. the question is: are you able to accept them and do so in a responsible manner?
in writing. everyone should pay their taxes properly, including large companies. tax evasion and avoidance lead to economic deficits which society then has to fill. that is why european rules are needed to combat tax evasion. companies operating in several countries should also be transparent and provide country-by-country information on the profits they have made and the taxes they pay on them. worldwide, at least 14 trillion euros have been stashed away in tax havens, two thirds of which are even in bank accounts in eu countries such as luxembourg and the netherlands. a large part of this money has been withdrawn from developing regions without paying taxes. that's extra grim because with that tax, twice as much can be done to end extreme poverty in the world. the tax evasion by multinationals therefore affects the poorest of the poor in this world and europe must act against it. the party for the animals does not expect juncker, former prime minister of luxembourg, to do this. luxembourg (like the netherlands, by the way) has always blocked european rules on transparency of taxes paid. is it likely that he will go to war against the tax haven he himself has created and maintained?

i would like to thank the rapporteur and the other rapporteurs who have contributed to these reports for their work. the recent years have shown that inadequate supervision of banks can have disastrous consequences for the stability of financial markets and the economy as a whole. with the launch of the banking union on 4 november and the direct supervision of the main european banks by the ecb, it is of the utmost importance that the ecb can act quickly, correctly and effectively when banks break rules which could lead us back to the practices and problems of before and during the financial crisis. that is why the subjects of these two reports are so important. in preparing and assessing these reports, we have been guided by the following three considerations: first of all, the strengthening of the financial sector. adequate supervision and addressing banking abuses are necessary to restore confidence in the european banking sector, both within europe and internationally. at the same time, the financial credibility and resilience of the sector must also be strengthened. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european parliament is able to vote on the amendments to the directive. the second point is to prevent financial instability. this means that investigations into bank infringements must be initiated and completed within a reasonable time. ensuring financial stability also implies that any decisions on sanctions or fines are published at the time when the results of those investigations are final. the last point i would like to mention is the improvement of the provision of information by banks to supervisors. we must do this as effectively as possible and we support the amendments that make this possible.

i would like to draw your attention to three aspects of unspeakable female suffering: the first. development of psychological support programmes for the many girls and women who have been raped by isis fighters; the second. the dire emergency of more than two million widows, religious "solutions" such as "mutaa ́a" (a "temporary marriage") or polygamy really need to be fought with iraqi human rights organizations; the third. finally, a recent iraqi law that no longer applies a minimum age for marriage but does set rules for girls as young as nine (!) who want to get divorced is very disturbing. the eu has also called for action to be taken in this area, together with iraqi human rights organisations.

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i must say i am relieved that finally the eu can become a party to the cites conference. i witnessed myself how important this is a few years ago when i was part of parliament’s delegation to the cites conference in doha. we did not speak with one voice, we were not very united, we were more occupied by our internal negotiations as eu member states and then we made a strong position at the conference itself, so i am very relieved that we have finally become a party to cites. another thing i would like to address here – and the commissioner has already mentioned it, as have many of my colleagues – is the importance of the fight against wildlife trafficking. that is something that should be done with cites but of course, and the commission knows this, this is something that should also be done much more strongly within the eu. i therefore hope that commissioner moedas can give the message not only to his colleague responsible for the environment but also to his colleagues higher up the hierarchy in the commission that we really need an eu action plan against wildlife trafficking, and the sooner the better.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, it was this weekend that i returned from lima where the climate summit took place, and i must say that i was impressed by the broadly shared determination to fight climate change. but the lima outcome also made it clear that we still have a very difficult battle against climate change ahead of us. let us face it: the world will emit more co2 tomorrow than today, and more the day after tomorrow than tomorrow. for that reason, it is crucial that public policy pushes investments towards a low-carbon economy. as regards the fuel quality directive, differentiation between different feedstocks is crucial, but the current implementing measure does not do this any more. so highly polluting nigerian crude or tar sand oils from other parts of the world are treated equally with much cleaner feedstock oils. that is against the objectives of the fuel quality directive, and for that reason i tabled – together with a few other groups – the objection. i am, therefore, extremely disappointed by the statement from the commission here today. i expected a much stronger commitment to the objectives of the fuel quality directive. i really do not understand, after seeing your 2011 proposal, how you can defend a completely contradictory proposal after the council rejected your first proposal. i really do not understand how you can defend both. it is absolutely clear that we have to steer investments, and we can only steer investments in the right direction if we encourage them in the right direction and discourage them from taking the wrong direction.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i do not think that any of us here, any of the parties that filed an objection to this proposal, are against the fuel quality directive. let me be very clear, we are strong defenders of the fuel quality directive. we think that, theoretically, this can be a very efficient tool. but that is the problem, we say theoretically, because with this proposal the commission is deliberately weakening the proposal. it is weakening the effectiveness of the proposal, and i say this also to my colleague julie girling, who is saying you can only object on legal grounds. if you read the impact assessment, with this proposal the commission is deliberately taking away one of the options to achieve the 6 % reduction. because of this proposal we can no longer differentiate between different feedstocks which is one of the three ways by which we can achieve the reduction. so this is literally taking away the efficiency of one of these measures, and that is what we are against. and this is very interesting, because if you read the impact assessment – and we are talking about better regulation, tomorrow the commission will present a programme on better regulation – the most efficient proposal should be the proposal from 2011. that is in the emissions impact assessment. so why is the commission not following its own impact assessment, not following its own proposal of 2011? has the world changed? no, if you read the impact assessment all the values are the same as in 2011. so we can only start to guess what happened, and indeed i come to the same conclusion as my colleague matthias groote. this is a consequence of the canadian lobby. probably all of you have been called or received e-mails from the canadians saying that we really should support this measure. why? because now we can no longer differentiate between the fuel types and therefore the door will be open to tar sands. and that, of course, is the biggest problem: we are defending the canadian lobby here. and let me say to the canadians, who are probably listening, this is not against canada. we just want to have differentiation between different fuel types. if the canadians can show they can produce a clean fuel type, even cleaner than just normal oil, then they should also get the credit for that. so you need differentiation between fuel stocks. if you are more polluting it should be counted as such. if you are less polluting it should be counted as such. that is what we call better regulation, and that is not in the proposal. this is really going against the core of the fuel quality directive. i agree very much with my colleague, gerben-jan gerbrandy. i was hoping that the commission would get the point, that the commission would realise that the fuel quality directive and its methodology is something that is important and should be preserved. however, the commission has only said we will review, we will organise a congress, and that is it. sorry, we really want to hear more. how is the commission going to make sure that after 2020 we will have a proposal that takes into account the differentiation between different fuel types so that the good ones are rewarded and the bad ones are not rewarded? that is what we are aiming for. and to my colleague from the epp who said that this will throw away five years’ work, i say no, it is this proposal that is doing precisely that because officially the commission is saying this methodology will end by 2020, and they are now just making an empty proposal. so if you really are concerned about the methodology then we should really fight for the proposal that was on the table in 2011 which does differentiate between different fuel types and which is even supported by epure – not always my friends when they do their biofuel lobbying, but they also see that they are no longer being rewarded and are therefore saying that this time they agree to us objecting to the proposal. and i think that is also important for my christian democrat colleagues. so therefore we object, and unfortunately, commission, you do not allow us to do anything other than to vote against this proposal, to object to this proposal, with pain in our hearts.
president. - i too would like to welcome the new commissioner, mrs bulc, to the plenary session of the european parliament. good luck and a pleasant cooperation in the years to come! we are discussing here today a list of serious violations in the transport sector. we all know that transport is a vital part of the european economy and we want to work with the juncker commission to improve the transport sector, make it fairer, ensure good competition, but also good social conditions for the workers. our group has come to the conclusion that this list is vague and incomplete. that's actually the main objection. the list is a good start in itself, but in the current economic conditions it is unclear and incomplete. this applies to both employers and employees. if employers do not comply with this list, they may lose their reputation as a reliable transport company. so those employers do have something to lose. and it must be clear to employers what they do and do not have to adhere to. the same applies to the employees. of course, there is a social aspect to this list. there are several elements of european transport policy which are not clear to workers at the moment: elements like reward, security, and you name it. so we want a new list. and i understand very well what the commissioner is saying - and what mrs meissner is also saying - that we are now delaying, creating confusion. but if the commissioner starts a new list early next year, we can re-approve it here before the summer recess and then everyone will know where he or she is. there is another element. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we all know that one of the great problems in european law at the moment is: "can we also maintain the matters we agree on here?" so, thanks for the heads-up. the list is not exhaustive, either for employers or for employees. please come up with a new list quickly so we can help the transport industry further.
president. - i too would like to congratulate the new commissioner. it is a newcomer, because parliament is rightly objecting to what is now being put forward by the commission. what is this about? the point is that a road haulage operator can lose his licence if he commits a serious offence. in my view, the list of serious infringements includes tackling unfair competition and social dumping. unfortunately, the commission does not think so. but the systematic abuse of the cabotage regulation and the posting directive is damaging european road transport. so it's really important that europe addresses that. for example, we could move towards a system where we make a real link between the usual place of work of an employee and the law applicable to his employment contract. of course we have to go for it. mrs. bulc, it's crucial that you consider that. i have also learned something from this whole process, namely that we should not deal with important matters of this kind through a comitology procedure, but through the normal regular legislative procedure. it's far too important for that. so, commissioner, we will not support you but we assume that the broad call of this debate to tackle social dumping and unfair competition is crystal clear to you.
mr president, in the netherlands we often say: "without transport, everything is at a standstill". today we can truly say: 'with the epp, s&d and a number of other parties, the transport sector in europe is at a standstill'. and that's a sad thing to see. is the european commission's proposal, in my opinion, perfect? my answer to that is no. it does not cover all possible infringements that carriers may commit and which would jeopardise their good repute. but this proposal does offer a very good first step in tackling the rogue transporters. we often draw up european rules in this house and then think that this level the playing field in europe. but that level playing field very often only comes about when the rules are actually implemented, enforced and sanctioned throughout europe. the commission proposal now threatens to be rejected. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. this brings me to a final point which is also closely related to this. the minimum wage is to be introduced in germany on 1 january 2015. this creates a lot of uncertainty for the european transport sector. international transport companies are very keen to have that clarity and my question to the european commission is that if transport companies actually complied with the rules, they would not be faced with all kinds of administrative hassle.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the six- and two-pack programmes. these are the two sets of measures introduced in recent years to extend and strengthen the stability and growth pact. the commission's evaluation of this is full of fine analyses but avoids the main point of discussion, namely whether and how much flexibility can be applied in maintaining budgetary rules. the commission has indicated that it will take a position on this in january. so today i can only rely on the omens of the past few weeks. and those omens are anything but good. president juncker himself said last week in a french newspaper that this commission sees the maintenance of budgetary rules as a political decision. the additional delay given to france last month to get its budget in order once again shows where this institution can lead. the french deferral is in fact contrary to the agreements which were agreed less than two years ago, less than a year ago, in the two packages we are debating today, the six-pack and the two-pack. in its own evaluation of these packages, the commission indicates that additional deferral is only allowed when a country has made good progress with reforms but is facing an unexpected economic setback. however, in its assessment of the french budget for 2015 at the end of last month, the commission makes it clear that france has done far too little to put the budget and the economy in order and to reform. so this delay was - as the commission itself indicates - not based on the budgetary agreements we have made with each other in the euro zone. the decision to postpone the vote is a political slap in the face. the first ten years of the euro have shown what happens when the rules of the stability and growth pact become a political game. what is needed in the budgetary rules is not 'more politics' or 'more discretion' or 'more flexibility'. instead, the new investment plan should be used as a back door to tighten the rules. only those countries that adhere to the stability and growth pact and only those countries that have implemented a majority of the country-specific recommendations should have access to the investment money of that plan. this means that countries are not only punished if they do not comply with the rules, but also rewarded with additional investment if they do. that is fair, efficient and clear and we are strengthening the budgetary rules, the european economy and confidence in the european union.
president, i also thank the vice-president of the european commission for his explanation of the matter this morning. it has already been criticised for the fact that the document we are talking about is short and factual. i congratulate him on that, because i think his analysis is very clear: either 'the six-pack and two-pack rules have worked', or 'we haven't had enough time to assess them in practice'. i 'll say: "if it ain't broke, don't fix it". that's what they say. we have drawn up these regulations in the previous period when the euro was hanging over the precipice. i think that this european parliament can be proud that we had the courage to take those steps back then. because if we had had the macroeconomic imbalance procedure, for example, from the very beginning of the euro - which is about imbalances in member states, let us be clear about that for a moment - then we might have been able to avoid the dramatic situations that we have seen arise in a number of member states. but what i think is most important is that we can talk about a text at this moment. the text is clear, but let us also look at the practical aspects. the british have a wonderful saying: 'the proof of the pudding is in the eating'. we can talk about a text, but at the same time, if the rules we are discussing here are already being drawn up in practice, then we are already in fact removing the foundations underlying this healthy euro. so the epp will also assess this commission on whether it is also keeping the agreements we made last term, which i believe were justified, with a number of member states. i will not name names, but we all know which country is the elephant in this conference room. the proof of the pudding is in the eating. do not just talk about the text, but show in practice that you support it. we shall judge the commission on that basis. "blue card" is the answer. yeah, it took a while for the question to come. it was more of an explanation. if he does not believe me, let me refer to the words of mr draghi. he says that if you want to be part of that euro, there are a number of agreements that go along with it. not because we want to be so mean or because we are number fetishists, but because those rules are the anchor of trust in our whole economy and the foundation underneath that euro. so if you say a, you have to say b. if you want to be in the euro, you have to keep your promises. it's as simple as that. and of course you look at the implications of that. but if you say "a", you have to say "b". one cannot exist without the other.
the six-pack and two-pack review comes at a politically sensitive time. the european community is not a mere institution, but a political one. 1) france, italy and belgium have structural problems with their public finances. unfortunately, the implementation of the agreed criteria is being delayed for political reasons. this inaccuracy further undermines the credibility of the euro area governance. 2) there are still countries in the eurozone and in the monetary union which do not really fit in. the recent desperate attempt by mr samaras and mr venizelos to call for early elections shows that a country like greece would be better off outside the euro zone. then the country with its own currency can devalue and, in combination with a partial debt write-off, really build with hope for the future. the country's current debt burden in the eurozone is heavier than the vault that titan's son atlas had to carry. i'm afraid the political elite here won't listen to my advice. the result is that we will be stuck with very high unemployment for many years to come, and that european economies outside the eurozone will perform much better, as they are doing now.
what if: it's bad weather, it's windy, the rain comes pouring from the sky and occasionally there's a flash of lightning from dark clouds. would you expect the plane to land on autopilot? no, of course not. and the european economy is still in bad shape. inflation is too low for households burdened with debt, unemployment is too high, growth is low and confidence is shaky. the commission has carried out a mid-term review of the budgetary rules in the two- and six-pack, but it is operating on automatic pilot. the bad weather for the european economy is not taken into account, let alone the question of whether the budgetary rules are appropriate in these bad weather conditions. and then one specific point. in the budgetary framework, the structural deficit is a very important concept. it's a great concept in theory, but a lousy one in practice. it's like flying with a broken altimeter. research shows that different institutions, the imf, the oecd and the european commission, give different estimates of the structural deficit and that over the course of the year the estimate can change considerably. this is contrary to the importance now attached to this concept and does not ensure a soft landing of the european economy.

on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, let me also congratulate president juncker on behalf of the alde group. first of all, we are talking better lawmaking today and in my view that would also mean transparency and indeed involving parliament, but to my knowledge we are having a debate today on the basis of a document that was leaked to the press. i find that an unusual procedure. alde naturally supports efforts to slim down and simplify eu legislation, but we believe in smart trimming, not in taking a blunt axe to the trunk of the tree. the commission should not throw the baby out with the bathwater by arbitrarily scrapping laws. the commission should respect the democratic policymaking process and not pick and choose. no one can be opposed to eliminating unnecessary regulation, but what one person considers unnecessary may be considered essential by someone else. the commission wants to concentrate on the big themes and alde, of course, fully supports that. now alde considers equal treatment a big theme, but in the commission’s logic the blocked anti-discrimination directive is unnecessary red tape. alde considers transparency a big theme, but in the commission’s own logic the access to documents law, blocked by the commission and the council, should be scrapped. alde considers sustainability a big theme, but the commission’s red-tape slayer wants to withdraw the proposals for waste and air quality. alde also considers upholding democratic values and the rule of law a big theme, and unfortunately in many member states it is a very topical issue these days, but the commission proposes no instrument. democracy and the rule of law are also necessary. conversely parliament rejected the proposal for an eu pnr scheme but the commission keeps pushing for it without any proper justification or evidence for its necessity. in our view the commission should focus on improving the process rather than ignoring the outcome of democratic decision. alde strongly objects to the withdrawal of files only and exclusively because the member states failed to take responsibility and the council is paralysed. if that were the rule, many good laws making the lives of eu citizens better would not have existed today. there would be no banking union, no european patents, no standards for the health and safety of workers in the internal market, no common asylum rules. scrapping or simplifying certain laws may be useful, but how will the commission tackle goldplating by member states, a major cause of excessive red tape? how will the commission ensure better implementation of eu laws by member states, and will the commission finally introduce a sunset clause in all legislative proposals. last but not least, will the commission drastically raise the standards of transparency and access to documents so as to allow for full accountability and public scrutiny, which in my view is key to better lawmaking? at his confirmation hearing vice-president timmermans said that this commission is born in the european parliament, so we expect the new commission to work in tandem with this house to reduce the regulatory burden but also to ensure a democratic and transparent legislative process.

the hostage situation in sydney by a supporter of the islamic state proves once again how topical the subject of security is. if we continue to look the other way and run away from the problem called 'islam', then the question is not whether this will happen in the european union but when it will happen again in amsterdam, berlin or london. and it's not just the returning jihadists or the supporters of the islamic state who pose a threat to our security. the biggest danger is the naivete, the structural denial of the dangers we face by the politically correct elite. the motion for a resolution before us today is a farce. i have three specific questions to put to the european commission. how many jihadists have left the european union to fight for the islamic state? how many jihadists have returned to european soil? and when are you going to acknowledge that islam is the biggest problem for our security? i'm going round. security is a national issue, but as long as the politically correct elite does not get its head out of the sand, the european union will not become more secure. the solutions are simple. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud, and it is not the commission's intention to do so.
mr juncker is coming up with a plan to boost eu growth. this plan means that the netherlands will have to steal another 8 billion from its own citizens to put into a structural fund. no doubt, then, airports are being built for which no dog comes, noise barriers are being placed along roads through uninhabited areas and absurd energy projects are being paid for. is mr juncker going to make shady backroom deals for this, as he did when he was prime minister of luxembourg? the initial 21 billion in eu and ecb contributions is to be increased to over 300 billion, mainly from the private sector. are there any good business benefits here, mr juncker? the commission still believes in the fata morgana of a great european empire, but the economic differences between the eu member states are too great. the price for this empire is paid by the citizens in the form of harsh austerity, staggering taxes, declining prosperity and rising unemployment. instead of a foolish growth plan, mr juncker should advocate in the european council the break-up of the eu and the euro. we must restore national sovereignty so that each member state can become prosperous again, can again have its own currency that fits its own economy, can again close its borders. then member states can keep out unwanted immigrants who cost billions a year and jihadists who threaten our security. mr juncker should ensure that, or he will have to resign.
the investment plan of the juncker commission is a game changer. but it is even more a change in political thinking than for the european economy. i see the investment plan as an extreme attempt by the commission to free up resources within the limited budget. but i see it above all as an invitation to the member states, to the council, to contribute further to the investment impulse. what we are asking from member states now is not just pretty words to support the plan, but also action. it requires a mutual effort. we ask countries with the financial room to contribute, and those with less financial room to make the effort to reform. and just so we 're clear , the budgetary rules should not prevent this, but rather promote it. only then will there be a much-needed boost to private and public investment. only then will there be a change for the european economy and for europeans.
in writing. the association agreement concluded between the eu and the republic of moldova in november 2014 has led to further tensions in the region. in response, russia announced a ban on imports of products from moldova. the european union is seeking to limit the impact on the agricultural sector by introducing autonomous trade preferences for the republic of moldova. but in my view this is not aid but a reckless retaliation against russia. the intensification of trade relations with moldova - and thus the increase in european influence in the region - has major geopolitical consequences. this proposal has been made carelessly and hastily and will further increase the conflict between the european union and russia. international security, human rights and democracy are always more important than short-sighted trade policy. that is why i voted against the association agreement in november and against the introduction of autonomous trade preferences for the republic of moldova today.
the european union has already concluded free trade agreements with colombia and peru, ecuador's neighbours. this has led to a deterioration in ecuador's trading position. by granting ecuador special tariff treatment, europe is seeking to limit the damage to ecuador. but the free trade agreements that europe is concluding cannot count on my support. this puts pressure on european production and safety standards, which may mean, among other things, a lowering of european standards for the environment and animal welfare. the current proposal also gives the european commission more power and reduces the influence of parliament and the member states. while i regret that ecuador is being the victim of unfair trade relations between europe and colombia/peru, i do not want a short-term solution in the form of tariff treatment for products from ecuador, when the consequences for animals, nature and the environment are not to be anticipated.
in writing. member states have moderated the increase in the eu budget for 2015. right, but the agreement still implies an excessive increase of over 1.8 percent over the 2014 budget, far above the low inflation. i cannot therefore support the budget. the negotiations were dominated by arrears in payments. it is necessary to address this. companies, research institutes and other beneficiaries must not fall victim to the eu's poor method of justification. europe promises more than it can deliver. it is planning too many financial commitments each year, which means that payments, with more realism, have to be made late, with a shortfall in funds when it comes to disbursements. only a reduction in financial commitments would really solve the problem of late payments. the european parliament, however, wants to commit and disburse more each year. it therefore has a great responsibility in this. the european parliament itself is responsible for the unrealistically high number of financial commitments which are not paid. whoever wants the bottom of the can gets the lid on their nose. parliament has not learned from this year, so the budget will increase again next year!
in writing. party for the animals opts for a two-state solution in the israeli-palestinian conflict both the palestinian and israeli states deserve recognition. we condemn any action by any party that could undermine the viability of and the prospects for a two-state solution and a lasting, just peace. the party for the animals rejects all forms of violence. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the illegal trade in human beings. we will assess proposals for the recognition of palestine on the basis of these principles. that is why i voted for the resolution recognizing palestine as a state.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all i would like this opportunity to thank senator feinstein for her perseverance and for seeing this report through before the change in the senate. i think this reminds us – and i think ms hohlmeier said it very rightly – that what sets a democracy apart from a dictatorship is accountability and moral authority. none of us around the world are immune to these tendencies. we all have this kind of violence in us, but what makes us different is that we have accountability and there is no impunity in a democracy. therefore i am very pleased to hear all colleagues from all political groups insisting on accountability and on the need for europe to come clean with regard to its role in this whole cia programme, because we can point at the americans, but europe has supported the cia counter-terrorism programme – all aspects of it. i am glad that poland finally made an admission, but let us not forget that there are a number of member states that are close allies of the us – the five eyes and the nine eyes – and this parliament has expressed itself several times. therefore i am actually shocked to hear the commonplaces uttered by the italian presidency. italy is the one country that has actually prosecuted cia agents. you have convicted 24 cia agents. will italy, will the italian presidency, insist on extradition by the united states and bring those people to trial, just as everyone else who is responsible for torture should be brought to trial? no, mr obama, we cannot draw a line under this until those responsible have been brought to justice. that is why we are a democracy and the others are not. i have another question: i read a statement in the press by the head of intcen, the intelligence centre which is a kind of vague, unclear, embryonic european union secret service. he said there is no way of knowing whether the intelligence that intcen receives was obtained through torture. excuse me? this is an eu body paid for by eu money, processing information that may have been obtained from third countries, processing information that may have been obtained from torture? well maybe he does not, but then we should know; we should finally get answers on the nature and the activities of intcen; answers we have asked for many times. we should have proper oversight over intcen. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer. – yes, i very much agree with you, it is not enough for the council – and that is not just the italian minister, that is all the member state governments – to make statements about the rule of law and human rights and all the conventions that they are bound to and the laws that we have. we had laws back then, but they were not respected. it is not good enough. those who break the law, even if they are governments, should be brought to justice. so yes, i very much agree with you.
mr president, i shall stick to the subject: "torture by the cia". the feinstein report is also a wake-up call for the european union. and i am happy about that, because the green group has been trying for the last few months to get the report that was made earlier in this house on the cia extradition flights back on the agenda. these are european countries that have been knowingly involved in the abduction of people and the interrogation of people in any way, not necessarily on american soil. somehow, the interest had waned. "sand over it", was my sentiment in that debate. but i understand this debate here today as a new beginning of an inquiry by the european parliament into the involvement of european countries in cia torture.
blue-card question. – i am glad that mr brok condemns the use of terror under any circumstances. even if it was understandable, if it was the circumstances, etc., is it not true, mr brok, that many years have passed when people should have been brought to justice and when there should have been accountability, but every single opportunity was missed until today? incidentally, when it comes to justice, the americans did manage to lock up the people who actually disclosed the scandals – for example mr john kiriakou, a former cia employee who spent time in prison – rather than the people responsible for terror. do you not agree that there was plenty of time to repair the mistakes that were made?
president, the us senate report has shown that prisoners have been tortured on a large scale by the cia in the post-9/11 period. of course, these suspicions have always existed, but now they have been proved beyond doubt. and while this has raised a lot of dust on the other side of the ocean, here in europe it is eerily quiet. the report does not explicitly refer to it, but it is no secret that european member states have been involved in these practices. this parliament, together with the council of europe, played a crucial role a few years ago in bringing to light the evidence that unfortunately our governments were also involved in illegal transfers, in making european airports available for torture flights and even in taking illegal detention centres into european territory. the same european union which rightly promotes the rule of law and fundamental rights as a crucial part of our community of values has itself been complicit in gross human rights violations during the heyday of the fight against international terrorism. the report calls on the us government to deal with the past and to learn lessons for the future. i would like to take this opportunity to ask the european member states the same thing. it's really time to get some clarity about the cia's cooperation. moreover, it is crucial for the eu's credibility as a champion of global human rights that we clear up this dark page in our recent history. i therefore also call on the commission to open an inquiry into violations of international law on european soil as a result of cooperation with the us intelligence services. the european parliament has called for this on several occasions, but it has been refused each time. the fact is that in a democracy, governments have to be held accountable.
mr. speaker, the senate select committee on intelligence report is a terrible summary of what the cia did to its 119 prisoners between 2001 and 2009. some of the most horrific examples have been mentioned here today, from waterboarding to sleep deprivation for 180 hours and rectal food ingestion. we are rightly appalled by this today. there are actually no words to express our horror, but we try. at the same time, many colleagues have rightly pointed out that it is a very important step for this senate committee to publish this report, a very important first step towards transparency and self-reflection. this self-reflection is essential if we are to prevent such excesses in the future. it would therefore be good if the full report of the senate committee were available soon. i also call on the eu countries to take steps in the field of transparency and self-reflection to provide such guarantees in europe as well. it is also interesting to note that this report only deals with the 119 prisoners who were tortured in the so-called black sites. what about the tens of thousands of prisoners who have been held in countries such as afghanistan or iraq and have been subjected to the same treatment? it would be good if we could also get more information and more transparency on that. finally, one last point. we live in times of unprecedented and unknown threats, and intelligence services are important to ensure our safety and the safety of our citizens. but we can never subordinate human rights to that important work. so let's not get bogged down in a debate that dick cheney and the cia might like to have about the effectiveness of torture. torture is unacceptable.
mr president, this concerns the agenda because we asked the council to make a statement about the matter that we have been discussing here all afternoon. i note that neither in its introduction, nor in its concluding remarks, has the council said anything about its role. so i consider there has not been a statement from the council about when they discussed the cia strategy at the time and discussed supporting it. so i consider that the council was not here, has not responded and did not make a statement.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, at the core of the accession process is the respect for the rule of law and fundamental rights. free and pluralistic media are an essential component of any democracy. i am therefore deeply concerned about what happened last sunday when media outlets were raided by the turkish police and critical journalists were arrested. at times when turkey was already coming in for criticism about press freedom and judicial independence, this counts as another blow. should the detainees be charged with alleged wrongdoings, we expect that a due process will be conducted whereby the principle of presumption of innocence will prevail, and an independent, transparent investigation will be conducted. last week i visited turkey as the european parliament’s rapporteur. there was a clear understanding from the side of the government that further reforms are needed. therefore i welcomed the statement by the government that the eu accession process, further democratisation and the kurdish settlement process are its first priorities. the fact that we are concerned about the deteriorating situation of fundamental rights and the rule of law in the country actually shows our commitment to the accession process. but in the end it is the turkish government which sits behind the steering wheel. the events of 14 december caused serious concerns in the eu about turkish willingness to make progress in the field of democratic reforms. in the weeks to come, therefore, it is up to ankara to convince us that it is still truly committed to moving closer to the eu and its core values. the accession process is a unique opportunity for turkey to develop a strong pluralistic democratic system with solid institutions for the benefit of all citizens of turkey. however, a crackdown on critical media is moving turkey further away from these core european values, and that is bad news for anyone who is a friend of turkey.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, our dream of a european turkey has turned into a nightmare and it is time for a wake-up call. i was among those critical friends of turkey who argued that an ongoing membership process helps reforms, but reality suggests the opposite. last weekend’s arrest of journalists is part of a trend of government-led breaches of fundamental freedoms and the rule of law in turkey. only a week after a high-level eu delegation visited ankara, relations have now hit another low point. there was reason for concern before. after corruption allegations included erdoğan – then prime minister, now president – and key members of his cabinet, hundreds of police officers and prosecutors were relocated and threatened with prosecution. those threats have now materialised and are also being used on journalists, while turkey already held the world record for imprisoned journalists. the obvious question is why the eu would act firmly only now. it comes down to our fears of turkey sliding ever further away from democracy if the negotiations were to stop. yet too many watershed moments have passed without serious criticism from europe, from the banning of an unpublished book by ahmet şik to mass arrests and trials without due process. the witch-hunt of gülen supporters is only the latest in a long list of rule of law violations. members of the fethullah gülen hizmet movement and the akp have created monsters together by helping each other in a coalition that long turned against everything on their combined path. now they are turning against each other, leading to even more violations of the rule of law. today there are masses of people in turkey who live in fear for their future – women, academics, artists, business leaders and, frankly, anyone who is critical of the government. losing a job or a licence, receiving a tax fine or even being prosecuted for attempting to overthrow the government have become common tools to punish opponents, while laws were passed placing members of the security establishment and government officials above those laws. it is time to tell the akp government that it is not business as usual. europe has to show that there is an end to the violations of the rule of law.
president, a country which imprisons journalists in this way and on this scale has no place in the european union. we all agree on that. it is indeed very remarkable that turkey is the only candidate country in the history of the european union that is not making progress but is falling behind in its approach to the copenhagen criteria and the values on which the eu is built. and that is also new for the european union. we have to ask ourselves how we should respond to that. we must of course condemn these actions in the resolution of january. but we must also ask ourselves whether we can justify leaving it to words alone. the dutch parliament this morning called on the european council to freeze or reduce pre-accession funds. but i also agree with mr pabriks that it would be important to use these pre-accession funds more effectively to provide extra support for civil society and the liberal forces in turkish society. so my question to the european council is: do you see any possibility of using these pre-accession funds to send a strong signal to turkey and at the same time to give an extra heart to the people in turkey who believe in europe and in european values?
i would like to express my full support for the approval of the association agreement with georgia. it is a logical and necessary step towards the economic, social and political reforms that the georgian people are clearly demanding. this is not the end, but the beginning of further cooperation with the eu. i therefore call for swift ratification by the member states so that deeper political cooperation and economic integration can be translated into action. this underlines the seriousness of the involvement of the european member states and also that the expectations of reform are not just on paper. the agreement anchors georgia's european course in terms of shared values. a pluriform political order, responsible governance, an independent judiciary and protection of fundamental rights. there is a government that is willing to work hard for a prosperous future. it is therefore their task to prevent polarization. civil society also plays an important role in this, both in supporting and in monitoring the necessary reforms. finally, i would like to express my concern about the recent integration agreement between russia and abkhazia, which may be followed by an agreement with south ossetia. the growing influence of russia in the border conflicts is a reality that we can no longer turn a blind eye to. it is therefore a good thing that the eu observation mission in georgia will be extended for two years.
president. - the next item is the continuation of the debate on the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on energy, research and technology, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the common organization of the market in natural gas. but what does this global issue of the first order have to do with our current debate on eu-georgia relations? let me be brief and clear in my answer: the activities of allegedly hundreds of iranian companies on georgian territory point to illegal acts that directly undermine the international sanctions regime against tehran and strengthen iran's controversial nuclear programme. my simple but urgent question to the commission is: what steps is the eu taking in close cooperation with the georgian authorities against these continued iranian nuclear proliferation efforts? how else will brussels keep up the pressure on tehran in the last crucial phase of the negotiations?
president. - that must have been a separate meeting, back in rome, with representatives of sudan and south sudan, egypt, ethiopia and eritrea. i don't see libya on the list, and i think that's because, by god, we don't know who we should ask in libya to represent a government. i remember debates in this house a few years ago about the deal that the italian government had made with libya so that libya would take in the migrants. and i remember the moment when mrs malmström, mr avramopoulos' predecessor, had to go to libya to ensure that 250 eritreans who had been sent back from europe (italy) to libya would not be sent back to eritrea, because that would be their death. we have sat here at the table to talk about "how do you keep your migrants at home?" with regimes that kill people! because that's what it comes down to. and we want to give those regimes that are killing people a hand by giving official development cooperation money. it is not just the name of the khartoum process, although i do wonder how you can start that process in the capital of sudan. i am concerned about the reprehensibility of this proposal and the way in which europe is packaging the return of migrants as development aid with dictatorial regimes.
the need to tackle the migration problem in a structural way, mr president, should be clear to us by now. as part of a holistic approach to migration, as we voted this morning in our resolution, we also need to integrate more closely the internal and external policies of the european union. it is good that the commissioner is also looking beyond the borders of europe to seek cooperation with third countries. but if you look at the map of africa and the countries that are participating in the khartoum process, there is a white spot between tunisia and egypt and that is libya. libya is not participating in the rome declaration in the framework of the rabat process. but libya is the main country of origin for people who want to cross the mediterranean and i wonder how we can make a european policy in this area without taking into account in any way what is happening in libya. how are you going to solve this, including in the context of the khartoum process?
mr president, a great deal can be said in just one sentence. the united democratic opposition in venezuela did that less than a week ago. her position is: the regime in our country can claim to have democratized impunity. that is a huge judgment. the venezuelan opposition deserves our full support in its legitimate quest for a democratic state of law in its own country. meanwhile, the maduro regime is distinguishing itself in a very different way, in a purely negative sense. the jewish community in venezuela is currently reduced to only 7 000 members, partly because of the brutal anti-semitism of the regime. increasingly, a situation of anti-semitism without jews is emerging in venezuela. what the left has to say about that, i'd like to hear.
